Large deviations analysis for random combinatorial partitions with counter terms
Stefan Adams and Matthew Dickson

Abstract
. In this paper, we study various models for random combinatorial partitions using large deviation analysis for diverging scale of the reference process. Scaling limits of similar models have been studied recently [ FS18a , FS18b ] going back to [ Ver96 ] . After studying the reference model, we provide a complete analysis of two mean field models, one of which is well-know [ BCMP05 ] and the other one is the cycle mean field model. Both models show critical behaviour despite their rate functions having unique minimiser. The main focus is then a model with negative counter term, the probabilistic version of the so-called Huang-Yang-Luttinger (HYL) model [ BLP88 ] . Criticality in this model is the existence of a critical parameter for which two simultaneous minimiser exists. At criticality an order parameter is introduced as the double limits for the density of cycles with diverging length, and as such it extends recent work [ AD21 ] .



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60F10; 60J65; 82B10; 81S40

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we study various models for random combinatorial partitions using large deviation analysis for diverging scale of the reference process.

1.1.
The reference measure

Random combinatorial partitions arise in many areas of mathematics as number theory, combinatorics, probability and statistical mechanics, as illustrated in
[
Ver96
]
and further developed in recent work
[
FS18a
,
FS18b
]
.

The problem is about decomposing an integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
into a sum of positive integers,
N=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m},m\in\mathbb{N}
. A partition of
N
is then the equivalence class of sequences
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
whose terms sum up to
N
and where two sequences are equivalent if they differ by a permutation. Following Vershik
[
Ver96
]
, we describe partitions by their
occupation
sequences
\lambda=(\lambda_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
\lambda_{k}
denotes the number of elements equal to
k
in a sequence representative of the partition, thus
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\lambda_{k}=N
. The
occupation numbers

\lambda_{k}
represent also the number of cycles of length in a permutation of
N
elements, in this way the sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
gives the cycle structure of permutations. We are concerned with random partitions models where we assign statistical weights for various models. All weights are so-called tilts of the reference measure. For the reference weights we denote
{\mathcal{N}}_{k},k\in\mathbb{N}
, the Poisson distributed occupation number with parameter
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}
, where
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert=(2N)^{d},d,N\in\mathbb{N}
, and

q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}=\frac{{\rm e}^{\beta k\alpha}}{(4\pi\beta)^{d/2}k^{1+d/2}}\,,\quad\mbox{ with }\alpha\leq 0,\beta>0,k\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(1.1)

The reference measure is then the superposition of all Poisson processes and is itself a Poisson process with parameter

\overline{q}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\;q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\,,

see, e.g.
[
Raf09
]
. We are concerned with diverging scales
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
and therefore introduce the
empirical cycle count
or the
empirical occupation count
as

\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}:=\big({\mathcal{N}}_{k}/\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,.

(1.2)

For a sequence
x=(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
with
\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert x_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we denote
\mathsf{Q}
the probability distribution of the reference process, that is, the probability that the empirical cycle count is equal to
x
is given by

\mathsf{Q}\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}=(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\big)={\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\overline{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\prod_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\big(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\big)^{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}{\big(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert x\big)!}\,.

(1.3)

The probability weights for the reference process are a special class of multiplicative weights. General multiplicative weights have been introduced by Vershik
[
Ver96
]
and are studied and analysed for scaling limits in
[
FS18a
,
FS18b
]
.
Our study is not concerned with scaling limits but with large deviation limits as
N\to\infty
. All our models are given as probability measures in the sequence space
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, and we denote
\nu_{N,\alpha}=\mathsf{Q}\circ\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}^{-1}
the reference measure on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. Before we introduce our tiled models in Section
1.2
, we motivate the specific weights of the reference measure. These weight appear in calculations of the trace of the Gibbs density operator for an ideal (non-interacting) gas of Bosons, a class of quantum particles obeying certain permutations statistics according to the representation of the permutation group of
N
particle indices. As identical quantum particles cannot be distinguished, one needs to symmetrise their labels. Using the Feynman-Kac formula one obtains the trace as an expectation for
N
Brownian bridges under symmetrised initial-terminal conditions, see
[
Ada08
,
ACK11
]
and
[
AD08
,
AK08
]
for the symmetrisation and random permutations and partitions. In the so-called
grand canonical ensemble
with random number
N
of particles we recover our reference weights for a special choice of the scales
\Lambda_{N}=[-N,N]^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and empty boundary conditions, see for instance
[
ACK11
,
AD21
]
. In Appendix
B
we summarise the results for the reference measure and well-know results for the ideal Bose gas and its condensation phenomenon.
In parts our work is related to
[
BCMP05
]
and
[
Lew86
,
BLP88
]
. However, it should be noted that in these studies the random weights for the partitions originate from an energy (Fourier space) representation of the underlying physical models. In this energy setting, condensation is concerned with the zero energy mode in the systems whereas in our study we are concerned with infinitely long cycles, see
[
Sü02
,
Ada08
,
AK08
,
ACK11
,
AV20
]
. Recently there is some work on random Euclidean permutations
[
EP19
]
which are different from the ones studied in this paper as their weights depend on the spatial distance as well. It can be promising to extend our models to include spatial dependence in the future.

1.2.
Models

Our models are given as probability measures on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
by various tilts of the reference measures. The tilts are so-called
Hamiltonian functions

H
on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that the new measure is given by the Radon-Nikodym density
\frac{{\rm e}^{-\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert H}}{Z_{N}}
with respect to the reference measure, where
\beta>0
and where
Z_{N}
is the normalising constant also called
partition function
. Under the given tilts the new measures put higher probability weight to cycle counts with lower values of the Hamiltonian function. We define two so-called
mean field
models and another model with counter terms.

For
a\geq 0
, define the
cycle-mean field model
(CMF),

\displaystyle H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\;x_{k}\right)^{2}\,,\quad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\,,

(1.4)

\displaystyle\nu^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{N,\alpha}({\rm d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm e}^{-\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(x)}}{Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha)}\nu_{N,\alpha}({\rm d}x)\,,\quad Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\left[{\rm e}^{-\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}\right]\,.

The measure
\nu_{N,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}
gives higher weight to cycle counts with smaller values of the total number of cycles. Any given cycle count is a partition of the number

\mathsf{N}_{N}:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}\,,

(1.5)

which we also call the ’
number of particles
’.
The number
\mathsf{N}_{N}
is only lower semi continuous and not upper semi continuous (see
[
ACK11
]
). For
a\geq 0
and any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, define the
particle-mean-field model
(PMF),

\displaystyle H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=-\mu\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kx_{k}+\frac{a}{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kx_{k}\right)^{2},\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\,,

(1.6)

\displaystyle\nu_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}({\rm d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}(x)}}{Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha,\mu)}\nu_{N,\alpha}({\rm d}x)\,,\quad Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha,\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\left[{\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}}\right]\,.

This models puts lower probability weight on cycle counts with large number of particles and has been studied in the literature, see
[
BCMP05
]
for a nice summary. The name refers to the number of physical particles in the system. The measure, though well-known in the physics literature, is different in character from the partitions weights studied in
[
Ver96
]
and
[
FS18a
,
FS18b
]
. Namely, as the earlier weights are just product of the single weights, the squared term in the Hamiltonian
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}
creates product of weights of pairwise different weight numbers.

The major novelty of our large deviation analysis concerns an substantial extension of the so-called HYL-model (
Huang-Yang-Luttinger model
) studied in
[
BLP88
]
. On one hand we replace the cycle weights originating from the energy representation in
[
BLP88
]
by our cycle weights stemming from spatial representation of the partition function, and on the other hand we obtain higher level large deviation principles allowing a detailed insight in the structure of the minimiser and possible phases and phase transitions. But significantly, we can dispense a major technical assumption in
[
BLP88
]
, see details on this in
[
AD21
]
.
For any
a\geq b>0
and any
\alpha\leq 0
,
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, define the HYL-model by

\displaystyle H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=-\mu\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kx_{k}+\frac{a}{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kx_{k}\right)^{2}-\frac{b}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}x_{k}^{2}\,,\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\,,

(1.7)

\displaystyle\nu^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{N,\alpha,\mu}({\rm d}x)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm e}^{-\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}(x)}}{Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{N,\alpha,\mu}}\,\nu_{N,\alpha}({\rm d}x)\,,\quad Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha,\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\left[{\rm e}^{-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu}}\right]\,.

1.3.
Organisation and summary of the paper

The papers is structured into four chapters and an appendix. The first two sections present our results and the remaining ones collect our proofs. The appendix has three parts; Appendix
A
introduces the Bose functions as a class of poly-logarithmic functions. In Appendix
B
we present a rigorous large deviation analysis of the reference process (ideal Bose gas) as courtesy for the reader and to present a new proof method using Baldi’s theorem
[
DZ09
]
. Appendix
C
defines the Lambert
W
function and collects some properties. This function is vital for the analysis of our rate functions for the CMF and the HYL model.
All our large deviation results are in Section
2
. The main focus is on large deviation results for the empirical cycle counts, for the reference measure and the PMF model we complement this with large deviation principles for the
empirical density

\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}:=\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\mathsf{N}_{N}\,.

(1.8)

Note that
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}=D\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}\right)
, where
D\colon\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)\to\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{+\infty\right\}
,

D\left(x\right):=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}kx_{k}\,,

(1.9)

which is given in Section
2.2
.
The large deviation principle for the CMF model in Theorem
2.1
uses standard large deviation methods. The major obstacle for the large deviation principles for remaining models is that the number of ’particles’
\mathsf{N}_{N}
or its density
D
is only lower semi continuous and not upper semi continuous. We prove the large deviation principle for the PMF model in Theorem
2.2
using a lower semi continuous regularisation of the Hamiltonian function following
[
GZ93
]
in conjunction with a finite-dimensional approximation. The proof of the large deviation principle of the HYL model in Theorem
2.4
combines the methods for the PMF with two different representations of the Hamiltonian function adapted to the lower and the upper bound of the large deviation principle, all of which is in Section
4.3
. The second main body of work is the variational analysis of the rate functions and models in Section
3
. We analyse the zeroes of our rate functions and derive representations of the so-called limiting
pressures
, that is, the limiting logarithmic moment generating functions of our models. We analyse the pressure functions as function of the parameter
\alpha
(CMF model) and
\mu
(PMF and HYL model) whose derivative give the density of particles. Critical behaviour is present when the derivative of the pressure is different from the expected density which is given by the density of the zeroes of the rate function.

For the CMF model we find that the unique zeroes are given as functions of the Lambert
W
function, and the zeroes and the analysis of the pressure show that the CMF model has similar properties as the reference measure including the so-called criticality in terms of
Bose-Einstein condensation
BEC defined for the ideal Bose gas in Appendix
B
. The difference is only in terms of the critical density which is now a function of the parameter
a
and the Lambert
W
function.

The corresponding analysis of the PMF model in Section
3.1.2
shows also unique zeroes for the rate function but this time the phase transitions establishes as a change in the pressure density relation in Proposition
3.6
and Proposition
3.8
. This leads to the conjecture that the
condensate density
is given by
(\mu/a-\varrho(\alpha))_{+}
, see for illustration Figure
2
, where the dashed red line represents the expected density and the horizontal line the derivative. Naturally, the analysis of the rate function for the HYL model is more complex, see Proposition
3.11
. Here, the Lambert
W
function plays a major role and uniqueness of the zeroes is only given for certain parameter regimes, see Theorem
3.12
. The main result concerns a critical parameter
\mu=\mu^{*}
in Theorem
3.14
when two simultaneous zeroes of the rate function exist. We believe that the zero with the lower density represents the system out of the condensate whereas the other one represents the system with condensation. The condensate itself is then the leftover probability mass and is conjecture to be the probability mass of the ’
infinitely long cycles
’, see
[
Sü02
,
Ada08
,
ACK11
]
. In order to shed some light on the condensation of cycles of diverging length we introduce in Section
3.2
an order parameter as a double limit of the density of diverging cycle length.

In future work we shall address scaling limits as in
[
Ver96
,
FS18a
,
FS18b
]
where we hope that the detailed analysis of the rate function zeroes can help establishing different scaling limits. Another question concerns the concentration of measure around the two distant rate function zeroes of the HYL model at criticality.

2.
Large Deviation Principles

2.1.
Large Deviations of the Empirical Cycle Count

We present our large deviation results which are all based on the large deviation principle for the reference measure
\nu_{N,\alpha}
in Appendix
B
. The large deviation principle and thermodynamic results are given in Appendix
B
. For the convenience of the reader and better understanding of our main results we present all details of the proofs of the large deviation principle and the exponential tightness using Baldi’s Lemma in Appendix
B
. For the following we recall the rate function for the ideal Bose gas, (see Theorem
B.1
)

I_{\alpha}(x)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{x_{k}}{\beta}\Big(\log\frac{x_{k}}{q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}-1\Big)+\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}/\beta\,.

From the construction of the CMF, PMF and HYL models via the ideal Bose gas model, it is natural to expect that their LDPs may be derived with an application of Varadhan’s Lemma. Nevertheless our results are more sophisticated because the tilts are not continuous in the latter two cases.

Theorem 2.1

(
Large deviations principle for CMF models
)
.

For any
d\in\mathbb{N},a>0
and
\alpha\leq 0
the following holds. The sequence
\big(\nu_{N,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}\big)_{N\geq 1}
satisfies an LDP on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
with rate
\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert
and rate function

I^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\alpha}(x)=H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}(x)+I_{\alpha}(x)-\inf_{y\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}\{H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}(y)+I_{\alpha}(y)\}\,.

(2.1)

Theorem 2.4

(
Large deviations principle for HYL models
)
.

For any
d\in\mathbb{N},a\geq b\geq 0,\alpha\leq 0
, and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
the following holds. The sequence
\big(\nu_{N,\alpha,\mu}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}\big)_{N\geq 1}
satisfies a LDP on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
with rate
\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert
and rate function

I^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}_{\alpha,\mu}(x)=I_{\alpha}(x)+H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(x)-\inf_{y\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)}\{I_{\alpha}(y)+H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(y)\}\,,

(2.4)

with

\displaystyle H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(x)

\displaystyle=H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}(x)-\frac{1}{2(a-b)}\left(\mu-aD(x)\right)_{+}^{2}

(2.5)

\displaystyle=-\frac{b}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}x_{k}^{2}+\begin{cases}-\mu D(x)+\frac{a}{2}D(x)^{2}&,D(x)\geq\frac{\mu}{a},\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}\left(-\mu D(x)+\frac{a}{2}D(x)^{2}\right)-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2(a-b)}&,D(x)<\frac{\mu}{a}.\end{cases}

In both, Theorem
2.2
and Theorem
2.4
, we have presented the lower semicontinuous regularisations of the interaction energy densities. It is this that allows us to overcome the lack of continuity in the original energy densities.

2.2.
Large Deviations of the Empirical Density

The large deviation principles for the empirical density require an independent proof as the contraction principle does not work directly. To see that,
recall that the ’particle‘ number
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}
is only lower semicontinuous and not upper semicontinuous, a proof via the contraction principle is only feasible if one considers cut-off versions of the empirical density
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}}=\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\sum^{K}_{k=1}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}
followed by analysing the limit
K\to\infty
for the corresponding rate functions. We do not follow this approach here and briefly outline a direct approach as follows.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\alpha\leq 0
, then for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the logarithmic moment generating function is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(t)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({{\operatorname{bc}}}})}}}\big[{\rm e}^{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert t\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}}\big]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}{\beta}\big({\rm e}^{\beta tk}-1\big)=\begin{cases}+\infty&,\mbox{ if }t>\lvert\alpha\rvert\,,\\
\in\mathbb{R}&,\mbox{ if }\alpha+t\leq 0\,.\end{cases}

(2.6)

The following large deviation results uses the critical density for the ideal Bose gas, the thermodynamic limit of the pressure and the free energy defined, respectively, in Appendix
B
. Denote
Q_{N,\alpha}=\mathsf{Q}\circ\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N}^{-1}
the distribution of
(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{N})_{N\geq 1}
with chemical potential
\alpha\leq 0
and define the distribution
Q_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}
via its Radon-Nikodym density

\frac{{\rm d}Q_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}Q_{N,\alpha}}(x)=\frac{\exp\left(-\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\beta\left(-\mu x+\frac{a}{2}x^{2}\right)\right)}{Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)}\,.

(2.7)

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta>0
.

(a)

For any
\alpha<0
, the sequence
\left(Q_{N,\alpha}\right)_{N\geq 1}
satisfies a LDP on
\mathbb{R}
with rate
\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert
and rate function

J_{\alpha}(x)=\begin{cases}p(\beta,\alpha)+f(\beta,x)-\alpha x&,\mbox{ if }x\in[0,\varrho_{\rm c}(d)]\mbox{ for }d\geq 3\wedge x\in[0,\infty)\mbox{ for }d=1,2\,,\\
+\infty&,\mbox{ if }x\notin[0,\varrho_{\rm c}(d)]\,.\end{cases}

(2.8)

(b)

For any
\alpha<0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, the sequence
\left(Q_{N,\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right)_{N\geq 1}
satisfies a LDP on
\mathbb{R}
with rate
\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert
and rate function

J_{\mu,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(x\right)=\begin{cases}J_{\alpha}\left(x\right)-\left(\mu+\alpha\right)x+\frac{a}{2}x^{2}-\mathsf{N}&,\mbox{ if }x\in[0,\varrho_{\rm c}(d)]\mbox{ for }d\geq 3\wedge x\in[0,\infty)\mbox{ for }d=1,2\,,\\
+\infty&,\mbox{ if }x\notin[0,\varrho_{\rm c}(d)]\,,\end{cases}

(2.9)

where

\mathsf{N}=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{J_{\alpha}\left(y\right)-\left(\mu+\alpha\right)y+\frac{a}{2}y^{2}\big\}\,.

Remark 2.7
.

The results in Theorem
2.6
make the heuristic derivations in
[
Lew86
]
rigorous and extend them to all
\alpha<0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.
The free energy of the PMF model is
f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\varrho)=f(\beta,\varrho)+\frac{a}{2}\varrho^{2}
, whereas the pressure

p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{(\mu+\alpha)x-\frac{a}{2}x^{2}-f(\beta,x)\right\}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{(\mu+\alpha)x-f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,x)\right\}\,.

(2.10)

The HYL model requires higher level empirical functionals as the energy cannot be expressed as a functional of the empirical particle density.

\diamond

3.
Variational analysis, pressure representations, and condensation

Our large deviation analysis in Section
2
is complemented by a complete analysis for the rate functions and pressure representations in Section
3.1
. In Section
3.2
, we finally study the onset of criticality know as the
Bose-Einstein condensation
(BEC) and discuss the relevance of our results.

3.1.
Variational analysis and pressure representations

The results for our reference measure are collected in Appendix
B
in Proposition
B.3
, Proposition
B.4
, and Proposition
B.5
. Both mean field models, the CMF and the PMF model, are closely related to the ideal Bose gas. Using our large deviation principles in Section
2
and the zeroes of the rate functions we obtain the thermodynamic limit of the pressure in our various models.

3.1.1.
CMF model

We collect the results for the first mean-field model. All proofs of this section are in Section
5.1
.

Proposition 3.1
.

(a)

The rate function
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\alpha}
has a unique zero at
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
given by

\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{k}=\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}\,,

(3.1)

where
W_{0}
is the real branch of the Lambert W function for non-negative arguments.

(b)

Let
\beta>0
,
\alpha\leq 0
and
a\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha)=\frac{1}{a\beta^{2}}W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{2}W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right)\right)\,.

(3.2)

Remark 3.2
.

Definition and properties of the Lambert function are given in Appendix
C
.

\diamond

Proposition 3.3
.

(a)

For
\beta>0
, we define
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha)=+\infty
for
\alpha>0
. Then
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\cdot)
is a closed convex function on
\mathbb{R}
.

(b)

For
\beta>0
,
\alpha<0
, the pressure
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha)
is smooth with respect to
\alpha
. In particular,

\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\alpha}=D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\right)=\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}D\left(q^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\right)\,.

(c)

In the thermodynamic limit
N\to\infty
,

\varrho^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\rm c}(d):=\lim_{\alpha\uparrow 0}\left(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\alpha}p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\alpha)\right)=\begin{cases}+\infty&,d=1,2\,,\\[6.45831pt]
\frac{W_{0}(a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}})}{a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}}}\varrho_{\rm c}(d)&,d\geq 3\,,\end{cases}

(3.3)

where
\varrho_{\rm c}(d)
is the critical density for the ideal Bose gas, see (
B.4
) in Appendix
B
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For
\varrho>0
, the free energy of the CMF model is defined as the Legendre-Fenchel transform of the pressure,

f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\varrho\right):=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\alpha\varrho-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha\right)\right\}=\begin{cases}\varrho\alpha-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha\right)&,\varrho\leq\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\rm c}(d),\\
-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,0\right)&,\varrho\geq\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\rm c}(d),\end{cases}

(3.4)

where
\alpha
is a solution to

\frac{1}{a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right)D(q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}})=\varrho\,,

which exists and is unique for
\varrho\leq\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\rm c}(d)
.

Remark 3.5

(
Conclusions CMF model
)
.

The CMF model shows similar results as the reference measure (ideal Bose gas) in Appendix
B
, e.g., the free energy is constant in the density beyond its specific critical density. The critical density of the CMF model is different from the ideal Bose gas one. Using properties of the Lambert function, see Appendix
C
, we know that

\displaystyle\lim_{c\downarrow 0}\frac{W_{0}(cx)}{cx}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\lim_{c\to\infty}\frac{W_{0}(cx)}{cx}

\displaystyle=0.

Hence, as the coupling parameter
a\to 0
vanishes, we obtain the critical ideal Bose gas density, and as
a\to\infty
the critical density decreases indicating BEC for much lower particle densities. Here, we refer to the definition of BEC for the ideal Bose gas as outlined in Appendix
B
. It is shown by
[
Sü02
]
and
[
BCMP05
]
, that BEC corresponds to loss of probability weights on finite cycles. When the coupling parameter
a
increases the number of finite cycles is suppressed in the probability measure, and therefore the system undergoes a transition to a regime where the particle density is realised in so-called infinite cycles. The CMF model has not been studied in the literature so far, it shows similar behaviour as the ideal Bose gas because the Hamiltonian adds only weight on large numbers of cycles present.

\diamond

3.1.2.
PMF model

We collect our findings for the PMF model. We obtain all results in
[
BCMP05
]
with a completely different method for all values of the chemical potential, in addition, we compute the condensate density in Theorem
3.17
below. We identify regimes where the expected particle density equals the density of the rate function zero or not.
All proofs of this section are in Section
5.2
.

Proposition 3.6
.

(a)

The rate function
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu}
has a unique zero at
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
where

\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{k}=q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\exp\left(\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta^{*}\right)_{-}\right),\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

and
\delta^{*}=\delta^{*}(\beta,\alpha,\mu,a)
is given implicitly as the unique solution to the equation

\delta^{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\exp\left(\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta^{*}\right)_{-}\right)=D(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})})\,,

(3.5)

\delta^{*}=\begin{cases}\in(0,\varrho(\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*}))&\,,\mbox{ for }\mu\leq 0\,,\\[4.30554pt]
\begin{cases}\in(\mu/a,\varrho(\alpha))&\,,\mbox{ for }\mu<a\varrho(\alpha)\,,\\
\varrho(\alpha)&\,,\mbox{ for }\mu\geq a\varrho(\alpha)\,,\end{cases}&\,,\mbox{ for }\mu>0\,,\end{cases}

(3.6)

where

\varrho(\alpha):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\,,\quad\alpha\leq 0\,.

(3.7)

(b)

Let
\beta>0,\alpha\leq 0,\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, and
a\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha,\mu)=\begin{cases}p(\beta,\alpha)+\mu^{2}/2a&,\mbox{ for }\mu\geq a\varrho(\alpha),\delta^{*}=\varrho(\alpha)\,,\\
\frac{a}{2}\big(\delta^{*}\big)^{2}+p(\beta,\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*})&,\mbox{ for }\mu<a\varrho(\alpha),\delta^{*}\in(\mu/a,\varrho(\alpha))\,.\end{cases}

(3.8)

(c)

For
\beta>0
and
\alpha\leq 0
, the pressure
p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}\left(\beta,\alpha,\cdot\right)\in C^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and is convex. In particular,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\begin{cases}\varrho(\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*})&,\mbox{ for }\mu<a\varrho(\alpha),\delta^{*}\in(\mu/a,\varrho)\,,\\
\mu/a&,\mbox{ for }\mu\geq a\varrho(\alpha),\delta^{*}=\varrho(\alpha)\,,\end{cases}

where
\delta^{*}
is solution of (
3.5
).

The following conclusion shows differences of the density as function of the dimension and
\alpha\leq 0
. The critical density
\varrho_{\rm c}(d)
is derived in (
B.4
) for the reference measure,

\varrho_{\rm c}(d)=\begin{cases}+\infty&,d=1,2,\\
\frac{1}{\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\zeta\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)&,d\geq 3\,,\end{cases}

where
\zeta
is the Riemann zeta function defined in (
A.2
).

Corollary 3.7
.

We have that

\varrho(\alpha)=\begin{cases}\in(0,\infty)&,\alpha<0,d\geq 1\,,\\
\infty&,\alpha\equiv 0\wedge d=1,2\,,\\
\varrho_{\rm c}(d)\in(0,\infty)&,\alpha\equiv 0\wedge d\geq 3\,,\end{cases}

In Figure

of the zero of the rate functions differs from the expected density which is represented by the dashed line. This signals a so-called condensate density which we will investigate further below in Section

. For

and
\alpha=0
, we do not have any critical behaviour as the density
D(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})})
is a growing function of
\mu
, see left hand side (A) of Figure
2
.

\mu
0
D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right)

(a)

d=1,2;\alpha=0

\mu
0
D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right)
a\varrho(\alpha)
\varrho(\alpha)

(b)

d\geq 3,\alpha\leq 0;d=1,2,\alpha<0

Figure 2
.
Total particle density of the zero of
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646135}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646135}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646135}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646135}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu}
. The limiting expected particle density (including the condensate) only differs for
\mu>a\varrho(\alpha)
, where it follows the dashed plot.

We conclude the analysis of the rate function deriving the free energy function from the pressure function.

Proposition 3.8
.

let
\beta<0
. For
\varrho>0
, the free energy of the PMF model is defined as the Legendre-Fenchel transform of the pressure,

f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\varrho\right):=\sup_{\mu\in\mathbb{R},\alpha\leq 0}\left\{(\mu+\alpha)\varrho-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu,\alpha\right)\right\}=f\left(\beta,\varrho\right)+\frac{a}{2}\varrho^{2}.

(3.9)

Remark 3.9
.

The free energy of the PMF model shows that the density square term in the definition of the measure stabilises the distribution and contributes towards the free energy.

\diamond

Remark 3.10

(
Conclusion.
)
.

The so-called BEC phase transition, see Appendix
B
, is established in various equivalent ways, in Theorem
3.17
below it is shown that the excess particle density is carried by so-called loops of unbounded length. Alternatively, Proposition
3.6
and Proposition
3.8
establish the phase transition via the change of the pressure density relation. The advantage of our LDP approach is that the rate function has unique zero and not an approximating sequence of minimiser. This is due to the fact that we are using the lower semicontinuous regularisation of the energy proving the large deviation principle. A close inspection of Figure
2

reveals this. For
d\geq 3
and
\alpha\leq 0
or
d\geq 1
and
\alpha<0
, we know that
a\varrho(\alpha)<\infty
, and thus the density of the zero of the rate function is constant for all
\mu\geq a\varrho(\alpha)
. In this region, the total particle density is the dashed line intersecting the point
(a\varrho(\alpha),\varrho(\alpha))
. The so-called condensate density is then
(\frac{\mu}{a}-\varrho(\alpha))_{+}
. This will be confirmed in Theorem
3.17
below.

\diamond

3.1.3.
HYL model

The zeroes and the analysis for the HYL model are more complex and involved. We collect different statements, the main ones are Theorem
3.12
and Theorem
3.14
for uniqueness and non-uniqueness of the rate function zeroes respectively. All proofs of this section are in Section
5.3
.

Proposition 3.11
.

(a)

The zeroes
\left\{\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\right\}\subset\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
of rate the function
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu}
satisfy the following expression,

\xi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}=-\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}W_{\chi^{*}_{k}}\Big(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp\big[\beta k\big(\mu-a\delta^{*}\big)\begin{Bmatrix}1&:a\delta^{*}\geq\mu\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&:a\delta^{*}\leq\mu\end{Bmatrix}\big]\Big),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

where
\left(\delta^{*},\chi^{*}\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\left\{0,-1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a solution to

\delta=g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right):=-\frac{1}{b\beta}\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{1}{k}W_{\chi_{k}}\left(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp\left[\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta\right)\begin{Bmatrix}1&:a\delta\geq\mu\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&:a\delta\leq\mu\end{Bmatrix}\right]\right)\,.

(3.10)

(b)

For
\mu\in\mathbb{R},\alpha\leq 0
and
a>b\geq 0
we the pressure is given by the rate function,

\displaystyle p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log Z_{N}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\mu,\alpha)=-\inf_{x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\big\{I_{\alpha}(x)+H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(x)\big\}\,.

(3.11)

Note that our results hold for all parameter
a>b
wheres the ones in
[
BLP88
]
apply only to
a=2b
, and we can dispense a technical assumption necessary in
[
BLP88
]
. Furthermore we are able to derive a full large deviation principle, whereas
[
BDLP90
]
was only able to find the pressure for their related model. We shall study the relation between the derivatives of the pressure and the expected densities. As this models has more than one potential minimiser (zero) of the rate function, we first single out uniqueness parameter regimes.

The next proposition shows that there exist two regimes for the parameter
\mu
depending on all other parameters such that the rate function has a unique zero. The zeroes of the rate function equal minimiser of the functional

F_{\mu}(x):=I_{\alpha}(x)+H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}(x)-\frac{1}{2(b-a)}\big(\mu-aD(x)\big)^{2}_{+}\,.

(3.12)

Theorem 3.12
.

For all
d\geq 1,\alpha\leq 0
and
\beta>0
, there exist
\mu^{+}(d,\beta,\alpha,a,b)\geq\mu_{-}(d,\beta,\alpha,a,b)
such that for
\mu<\mu_{-}
and
\mu>\mu^{+}
the function
F_{\mu}
has a unique minimiser, corresponding to the parameter
(\delta,0)
where
\delta
is the unique solution to the equation

\delta=g^{\chi\equiv 0}(\delta)\,,

where
g^{\chi}
is defined in (
3.10
).

Theorem
3.12
establishes parameter regimes for which the rate function has a unique zero and thus no phase transition or critical behaviour is present.
In these ‘unique zero’ regimes, we have an expression for the derivative of the thermodynamic pressure.

Proposition 3.13
.

(a)

For the regime described in Theorem
3.12
, i.e., for
\mu<\mu_{-}
and
\mu>\mu^{+}
, the pressure
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha,\mu\right)
is smooth and convex in
\mu
. In particular,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\begin{cases}D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\right)&,\mu<\mu_{-}\\
\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\right)\right)&,\mu>\mu_{+},\end{cases}

for such
\mu
.

(b)

The zeroes
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}
in the uniqueness regime
\mu<\mu_{-}
and
\mu>\mu^{+}
are
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
where

\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{k}=-\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}W_{0}\left(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp\left[\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta^{*}\right)\right]\right),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

and
\delta^{*}=\delta^{*}(\beta,\mu+\alpha,a,b)
is given implicitly as the unique solution to the equation
\delta^{*}=g^{0}\left(\delta^{*}\right)
,
where
g^{0}=g^{\chi\equiv 0}
.

The remaining crucial question is whether within the other parameter regimes one can identify parameter values with multiple zeros of the rate function. This in turn signals critical behaviour and is of fundamental interest.

Before we present our non-uniqueness results in the next theorem we collect some facts about the possible solutions for
\chi\equiv 0
. Recall that then

\displaystyle\xi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}W_{0}(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp(\mathscr{E}_{k}(\delta))\big)\,,\quad\mbox{where }\delta\mbox{ solves}

(3.13)

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=g^{0}(\delta)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{k}\,,

and

\mathscr{E}_{k}(\delta)=\beta k\big(\mu-a\delta^{*}\big)\begin{Bmatrix}1&:a\delta^{*}\geq\mu\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&:a\delta^{*}\leq\mu\end{Bmatrix}\leq 0\,\mbox{ and }\mathscr{E}_{k}(\delta)\uparrow 0\mbox{ as }\delta\uparrow\mu/a\mbox{ and }\mathscr{E}_{k}(\delta)\to-\infty\mbox{ as }\delta\to\infty\,.

The solution in (
3.13
) is only well-defined as long as

0\geq-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp(\mathscr{E}_{k}(\delta))\geq-{\rm e}^{-1}\,,\quad\mbox{ for all }k\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(3.14)

In case (
3.14
) fails for some
k
we have no solution for that value of
\delta
, and hence no stationary point and zero. The condition (
3.14
) is satisfied for every
\delta
as long as

\beta\geq\beta^{*}:=\Big(\frac{({\rm e}b)^{2}}{(4\pi)^{d}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{d-2}}\quad\text{ or equivalently }\quad b\leq\frac{1}{{\rm e}\beta q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}=\frac{(4\pi\beta)^{d/2}}{{\rm e}\beta}=:b^{*}\,.

(3.15)

The cases
d=1,2
are noteworthy: in
d=2
the upper bound for
b
does not depend on
\beta
whereas in
d=1
that upper bound is a decreasing function of
\beta
.

In order to formulate the conditions for different cases we introduce the parameters
\mu_{\rm p}
and
\mu_{\rm tang}
. To define them, it will be convenient to define the function
\tilde{h}\colon(-\infty,0]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
as

\tilde{h}(x):=-\frac{1}{b\beta}\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{1}{k}W_{\chi_{k}}\left(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp\left(\frac{ab}{a-b}\beta kx\right)\right).

(3.16)

Note that
g^{0}(\delta)=\tilde{h}(\delta-\frac{\mu}{a})
for
\delta\leq\frac{\mu}{a}
. We then define
\mu_{\rm p}
as the chemical potential that produces a solution to (
3.13
) at the peak of
g^{0}(\delta)
- represented pictorially in Figure
3
. This value is given by

\mu_{\rm p}:=a\tilde{h}(0).

(3.17)

Then there is only an unique solution to
\delta=g^{0}(\delta)
possible for any
\mu>\mu_{\rm p}
, and this solution is for small values of
\delta
.
Note that
\mu_{\rm p}=\mu_{\rm p}(b)
is increasing in
b\leq b^{*}
. For values of
\mu<\mu_{\rm p}
, the peak wanders to the left and lies above the diagonal identity line. The blue line in Figure
3
may have up to three intersections with the graph
g^{0}
(red line). Now define

\mu_{\rm tang}:=a\inf_{x\leq 0}\big\{\tilde{h}(x)-x\big\}\,.

(3.18)

At
\mu=\mu_{\rm tang}
there is a tangential intersection whereas for
\mu<\mu_{\rm tang}
there is a unique intersection at some
\delta^{*}>\mu/a
. We see from the construction that
\mu_{\rm tang}\leq\mu_{\rm p}
. In particular, if
\lim_{x\uparrow 0}\tilde{h}^{\prime}(x)>1
then the inequality is strict. This is always the case for
d=3,4
which can be seen calculating the derivative. For the remaining cases
d\geq 5
we need the assumption (
3.19
) below.

Figure
3
demonstrates how up to three possible minimisers can exist. There are three cases to distinguish. (i) The blue line intersects the red line only once. This happens for
\mu<\mu_{\rm tang}
at some
\delta^{*}>\mu/a
and for
\mu>\mu_{\rm p}
at some
\delta^{*}<\mu/a
. This case is the unique regime in Theorem
3.12
above. (ii) For
\mu=\mu_{\rm tang}
the blue line is tangent to
g^{0}
on the left hand side of the peak and intersects
g^{0}
to the right hand side of the peak. Likewise for
\mu=\mu_{\rm p}
, the blue line intersects once to the left hand side of the peak and once to the right hand side of the peak. (iii) For
\mu\in(\mu_{\rm tang},\mu_{\rm p})
, the blue line has two intersections with
g^{0}
to the left hand side of the peak and one with
g^{0}
to the right hand side of the peak.

In the context of the following theorem, for
d\geq 5
the following condition on the
\chi=0
solutions is required.

\lim_{\delta\uparrow\frac{\mu}{a}}\big(g^{0}\big)^{\prime}(\delta)\equiv\lim_{x\uparrow 0}h^{\prime}(x)>1\,\quad\mbox{ for }\mu\leq\mu_{\rm p}\,.

(3.19)

If this condition does not hold, then the behaviour is qualitatively different. In particular, the simultaneous minimiser behaviour in the following theorem does not occur.

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
d\geq 3,\beta>0,a>0
and
0<b<\min\big\{a,(\beta q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})})^{-1}{\rm e}^{-\beta\mu_{\rm p}/a}\big\}
. If
d\geq 5
, suppose that (
3.19
) also holds.
Then there exist a unique
\mu^{*}\in(\mu_{\rm tang},\mu_{\rm p})
, such that
F_{\mu}
has a unique minimiser for
\mu<\mu^{*}
and for
\mu>\mu^{*}
, and two simultaneous minimiser at
\mu=\mu^{*}
. In particular,
\inf\{F_{\mu}\}
is differentiable on
(-\infty,\mu^{*})\cup(\mu^{*},\infty)
, and not on any open neighbourhood of
\mu^{*}
.
The densities of the two simultaneous minimisers
\xi^{1}(\mu^{*})
and
\xi^{2}(\mu^{*})
satisfy
D(\xi^{1}(\mu^{*}))>\mu^{*}/a>D(\xi^{2}(\mu^{*}))
.

Remark 3.15
.

This theorem proves our conjectures above. The critical value
\mu^{*}
for the two simultaneous minimiser is not know explicitly but we can see that for dimensions
d\geq 3
, the value decreases with increasing the parameter
\beta
. If we increase the parameter
b
of the negative counter terms in the measure (Hamiltonian) we obtain smaller values in the critical parameter as well. This behaviour of the system is been expected and our result establishes a proof for the whole parameter regime
b\in[0,b^{*})
.

\diamond

\delta
g^{0}\left(\delta\right)
\frac{\mu_{\rm p}}{a}

0
\delta
g^{0}\left(\delta\right)
\frac{\mu_{\rm tang}}{a}

Figure 3
.
Sketch of
g^{0}\left(\delta\right)
against the diagonal
g^{0}\left(\delta\right)=\delta
for
d=3,4
,
\beta\geq\beta^{*}
and
\mu=\mu_{\rm p}
and
\mu=\mu_{\rm tang}<\mu_{\rm p}
. The chemical potential
\mu_{\rm p}
is defined so that there is an intersection at the peak of
g^{0}\left(\delta\right)
, and
\mu_{\rm tang}
is defined so that there is a tangential intersection. Note that
g^{0}\left(\delta\right)
translates with
\mu
.

3.2.
Criticality and Bose-Einstein condensation

The results in Section
3.1
signal three different types of criticality in our analysis. First the so-called BEC according to the reference measure and the CMF model, see Appendix
B
and Section
3.1.1
: The reference measure and the CMF model show some critical behaviour, namely, the density and chemical potential relation breaks down in dimensions
d\geq 3
. The excess density above the critical value of the density is identified as the BEC condensate density. The free energy is constant for all densities above the critical one, showing that the condensate density does not contribute to the free energy. In
[
BCMP05
]
, using the energy (Fourier) representation, it is shown that the excess density for the reference measure (for dimensions
d\geq 3
) equals the expected density of particles in the zero-energy mode. We believe that the same holds for our CMF model.

For the PMF model in Section
3.1.2
critical behaviour is the change in the derivative of the pressure-density relation, see Figure
2
, where the dashed line in (B) shows the density and signals that the excess density is given by
(\frac{\mu}{a}-\varrho(\alpha))_{+}
