H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu}(x)=H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,a}(x)+H_{-}(x)\geq H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,a,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(x)+H_{-}(x)\geq\big(H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,a,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}+H_{-}\big)_{\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(x)
. To show (
4.25
) we use the proof of Lemma
4.7
and choose the sequence according to (
4.22
). We thus obtain
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(x)\geq\big(H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,a,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}+H_{-}\big)_{\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(x)
.
∎

We finally combine Proposition
4.8
and Proposition
4.9
to finish the proof for Theorem
2.4
.∎

4.4.
Proof of Theorem
2.6

To prove the first part of Theorem
2.6
requires the limiting logarithmic moment generating function in Proposition
2.5
and an application of the Gärtner-Ellis theorem.

Proof of Proposition
2.5
.
For
t\in\mathbb{R}
we get, using the independence of the Poisson point processes,

{\tt E}\big[{\rm e}^{\beta t\sum_{k+1}^{\infty}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}\big]=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}{\tt E}\big[{\rm e}^{\beta t\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}\big]=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{\beta tkm}\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{N}}_{k}=m)=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}({\rm e}^{\beta tk}-1)},

and thus

\mathcal{L}(t)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log{\tt E}\big[{\rm e}^{\beta t\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}\big]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}{\beta}\big({\rm e}^{\beta tk}-1\big).

∎

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.
(a) This is a straightforward application of the Gärtner-Ellis theorem, see
[
DZ09
]
. To ensure that
0
is in the domain of the logarithmic moment generating function we need to have
\alpha<0
. Then the rate function is giving as the Legendre-Fenchel transform

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{tx-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}{\beta}\big({\rm e}^{\beta tk}-1\big)\big\}

\displaystyle=\big(p\left(\beta,\alpha\right)+\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{(t+\alpha)x-p(\beta,\alpha+t)\big\}-\alpha x\big)

\displaystyle=\big(p\left(\beta,\alpha\right)+f\left(\beta,x\right)-\alpha x\big)\,,

where we used that for
x\leq\varrho_{\rm c}
,

f(\beta,x)=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\{\alpha x-p(\beta,\alpha)\}.

Clearly,
J_{\alpha}(x)=\infty
when
x<0
as the empirical density only takes positive values. Suppose that
x>\varrho_{\rm c}
for dimensions
d\geq 3
(
\varrho_{\rm c}=\infty
for
d=1,2
). Then

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\left(t+\alpha\right)\varrho_{\rm c}+\left(t+\alpha\right)\left(x-\varrho_{\rm c}\right)-p\left(\beta,\alpha+t\right)\right\}\geq f\left(\beta,\varrho_{\rm c}\right)+\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\left(t+\alpha\right)\left(x-\varrho_{\rm c}\right)\right\}=+\infty\,,

(4.26)

and thus
J_{\alpha}(x)\equiv+\infty
for
x\notin[0,\varrho_{\rm c}]
.

(b) This is straightforward application of Varadhan’s Lemma in
[
DZ09
, Theorem 4.3.1]
using that
h(x):=x\mapsto-\mu x+\frac{a}{2}x^{2}
is continuous and the fact that the tail-condition is satisfied

\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log{\tt E}\big[{\rm e}^{-\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert h(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{n})}{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{h(\boldsymbol{\rho}_{n})\geq M\}\big]=-\infty.

∎

5.
Variational analysis, pressure representation and condensation - Proofs

This section collect all variational analysis proofs. We use frequently the following technical lemma for calculating derivatives of the rate functions.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an open interval,
F:\ell_{1}\times I\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\xi\in C^{1}\left(I;\ell_{1}\right)
. Also define

\mathcal{G}:I\to\mathbb{R};\quad s\mapsto F\left(\xi\left(s\right),s\right).

Then if
F\left(x,s\right)
is Gâteaux differentiable in its first argument at
\xi\left(s\right)
with
\left.\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{k}}\right|_{\xi}=0

\forall k\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\frac{{\rm d}\mathcal{G}}{{\rm d}s}=\left.\frac{\partial F}{\partial s}\right|_{\xi}.

Proof.
An application of the chain rule gives
\frac{{\rm d}\mathcal{G}}{{\rm d}s}=\left.\frac{\partial F}{\partial s}\right|_{\xi}+\sum^{\infty}_{j=1}\frac{{\rm d}\xi_{k}}{{\rm d}s}\left.\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{k}}\right|_{x=\xi}
. Since the partial derivatives of
F
with respect to
x_{k}
vanish at
\xi
, we only keep the first term.
∎

5.1.
Proofs for the CMF model

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.
Recall that
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}_{\alpha}
is lower semicontinuous and has compact level-sets. Also note that
I_{\alpha}
is strictly convex where it is finite, and that
H^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}
is convex. Therefore
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha}
is strictly convex where it is finite (a non-empty set) and uniqueness of the minimiser follows.

To calculate the minimiser, we search for stationary points. Since
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}
is strictly convex where it is finite, if we find a stationary point then it is the global minimiser. By considering the coordinate derivatives, we know that the minimiser must satisfy all the following equations

\frac{1}{\beta}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}+a\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}x_{k}=0,\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

To make this more manageable, we introduce the dummy variable
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and corresponding equation
\Gamma=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}x_{k}
. We shall solve

\displaystyle\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}+a\beta\Gamma

\displaystyle=0,\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

(5.1)

\displaystyle\Gamma-\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}x_{k}

\displaystyle=0.

(5.2)

Given
\Gamma
, (
5.1
) is uniquely solved by
x_{k}=q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}\exp\left(-a\beta\Gamma\right),k\in\mathbb{N}
, and therefore (
5.2
) becomes

\Gamma=\exp\left(-a\beta\Gamma\right)\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}.

This has the unique solution
\Gamma=\frac{1}{a\beta}W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right)
,
and so (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) are uniquely jointly solved by
x=\xi
given by

\xi_{k}=\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.
(a) The continuity of
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}
for
\alpha\leq 0
follows from (
3.2
) and the continuity of
a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}
and
W_{0}
. Convexity follows from considering the derivatives of
W_{0}
with respect to
\alpha
for
\alpha<0
, see Appendix
C
for derivatives of the Lambert function.

(b) Smoothness follows from
W_{0}
and the Bose functions being differentiable on the appropriate regions. The form of the first derivative can be found by either directly differentiating (
3.2
), or by using Lemma
5.1
with the zero found in Proposition
3.1
.

(c) We obtain (
3.3
) from (
B.4
) and the continuity of
W_{0}
and
\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.
This is proven in the same way as Proposition
B.5
.
∎

5.2.
Proofs for the PMF model

We collect our proofs for the PMF model.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.
(a) To obtain the unique zero of the rate function we shall find the unique minimiser of the un-normalised rate function
F(x):=I_{\alpha}(x)+H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(x)
. For the existence of a minimiser, recall that
F
is lower semicontinuous and has compact level-sets. Also note that
I_{\alpha}
is strictly convex where it is finite, and
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}
is also convex in the linear function
D(x)
. Therefore
F
is strictly convex where it is finite ( a non-empty set) and uniqueness of the minimiser follows. To calculate the minimiser, we search for stationary points. Since
F
is strictly convex where it is finite, if we find a stationary point then it is the global minimiser. By considering again as in the proof of Proposition
B.3
the coordinate derivatives, we know that the minimiser must satisfy all the following equations

\frac{1}{\beta}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}+k\left(aD(x)-\mu\right)_{+}=0,\quad k\in\mathbb{N}\,.

To make this more manageable, we introduce the dummy variable
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and corresponding equation
\delta=D(x)
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}+k\left(a\delta-\mu\right)_{+}

\displaystyle=0,\quad k\in\mathbb{N}\,,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\delta-D(x)

\displaystyle=0.

(5.4)

Given the value
\delta
, (
5.3
) is uniquely solved by
x_{k}=q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\exp\left(\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta\right)_{-}\right),k\in\mathbb{N}\,,
and therefore (
5.4
) becomes

\delta=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\exp\left(\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta\right)_{-}\right)\,.

(5.5)

Denote the right hand side in (
5.5
) by
h(\delta)
, and note that
h(\delta)\to 0
as
\delta\to\infty
. Furthermore,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}h(\delta)=\begin{cases}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha+\mu}})}}=:\varrho(\alpha+\mu)\in(0,\infty)&,\mu<0,\\
\varrho(\alpha)&,\mu\geq 0\,,\end{cases}

with

\varrho(\alpha)=\begin{cases}\in(0,\infty)&,\alpha<0,d\geq 1\,,\\
\infty&,\alpha\equiv 0\wedge d=1,2\,,\\
\varrho_{\rm c}(d)\in(0,\infty)&,\alpha\equiv 0\wedge d\geq 3\,,\end{cases}

see Figure
4
. In all cases there exists a unique solution which we denote
\delta^{*}
. For
\mu\leq 0
the solution is
\delta^{*}\in(0,\varrho(\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*}))
, and for
\mu>0
we have two additional cases, that is,

\delta^{*}=\begin{cases}\in(0,\varrho(\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*}))&,\mbox{ for }\mu\leq 0\,,\\
\begin{cases}\in(\mu/a,\varrho(\alpha))&\,,\mbox{ for }\mu<a\varrho(\alpha)\,,\\
\varrho(\alpha)&\,,\mbox{ for }\mu\geq a\varrho(\alpha)\,,\end{cases}&,\mbox{ for }\mu>0\,.\end{cases}

0
h\left(\delta\right)
\delta
\frac{\mu}{a}

(a)

d=1,2

0
h\left(\delta\right)
\delta
\frac{\mu}{a}
\varrho(\alpha)

(b)

d\geq 3

Figure 4
.
Sketch of
h\left(\delta\right)
. This shows
\mu>0
, but the sketch translates with
\mu
.

(b) For the thermodynamic pressure we use the large deviation rate function and obtain, using the unique zero in (a),

\displaystyle p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha,\mu)

\displaystyle=-\inf_{y\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\big\{I_{\alpha}(y)+H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(y)\big\}

\displaystyle=p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}(\beta,\alpha,\mu)=\begin{cases}p(\beta,\alpha)+\mu^{2}/2a&,\mbox{ for }\mu\geq a\varrho(\alpha),\delta^{*}=\varrho(\alpha)\,,\\
\frac{a}{2}\big(\delta^{*}\big)^{2}+p(\beta,\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*})&,\mbox{ for }\mu<a\varrho(\alpha),\delta^{*}\in(\mu/a,\varrho(\alpha))\,.\end{cases}

(c) This follows from either directly differentiating (
3.8
), or by using Lemma
5.1
with the zero found in (a). Convexity also follows from (a) and (b), noting that
D\left(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\right)
is continuous and increasing in
\mu
. ∎

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.
For
\mu\geq a\varrho(\alpha)
we have

\frac{{\rm d}p^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}}{{\rm d}\alpha}=\varrho(\alpha)\,\mbox{ and }\,\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\frac{\mu}{a}=\delta^{*}=\varrho(\alpha)\,.

Thus
\mu\varrho(\alpha)-\mu^{2}/2a=\frac{a}{2}\varrho(\alpha)^{2}
, and then the supremum over
\alpha\leq 0
gives the free energy for the ideal Bose gas and therefore the statement for this case. For the remaining case
\mu<a\varrho(\alpha)
, we note that

\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\alpha}=\frac{{\rm d}p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\varrho(\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*})

with
\delta^{*}\in(\mu/a,\varrho(\alpha))
. Thus

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\leq 0,\mu\in\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\big\{(\alpha+\mu)\varrho(\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*})-a/2(\delta^{*})^{2}-p(\beta,\alpha,\mu-a\delta^{*})\big\}=

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\leq 0,\mu\in\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\big\{(\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*})\varrho(\alpha+\mu-a\delta^{*})+a(\delta^{*})^{2}-\frac{a}{2}(\delta^{*})^{2}-p(\beta,\alpha,\mu-a\delta^{*})\big\}=f(\beta,\varrho)+a/2\varrho^{2}\,.

∎

5.3.
Proofs variational analysis of the HYL model

We present the variational analysis proofs for the HYL model.

Proof of Proposition
3.11
.
(a) Suppose that the partial derivative
\frac{\partial I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu}}{\partial x_{k}}
is defined and non-zero at
x\in\mathrm{Int}\left\{\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)\right\}
. Then
I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu}
does not achieve its infimum at
x
. Since the boundary
\partial\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)=\left\{x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right):\exists\,k\text{ s.t. }x_{k}=0\right\}
, and
\frac{\partial I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu}}{\partial x_{k}}=-\infty
here, the infimum is not achieved here.

For
x\in\mathrm{Int}\left\{\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)\right\}
we have

\frac{\partial I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu}}{\partial x_{k}}(x)=\beta^{-1}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}-bk^{2}x_{k}-k\left(\mu-aD\left(x\right)\right)\begin{Bmatrix}1&,aD\left(x\right)\geq\mu\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&,aD\left(x\right)\leq\mu\end{Bmatrix},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

which is defined everywhere in
\mathrm{Int}\left\{\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)\right\}
. Hence a solution
\xi
must solve
\frac{\partial I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu}}{\partial x_{k}}(\xi)=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. To make this more manageable, we introduce the dummy variable
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and corresponding equation
\delta=D\left(x\right)
. Our problem is then to solve

\displaystyle\log\frac{x_{k}}{q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}-b\beta k^{2}x_{k}-\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta\right)\begin{Bmatrix}1&,a\delta\geq\mu\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&,a\delta\leq\mu\end{Bmatrix}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

(5.6)

\displaystyle\delta-D\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=0.

(5.7)

Unfortunately - unlike in the corresponding PMF case - even when we are given
\delta
we are not guaranteed to have a solution for (
5.6
), or that such a solution would be unique. If we fix
\delta
, then the
k^{th}
equation of (
5.6
) either has no solution or is solved by

x_{k}=-\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}W_{\chi_{k}}\Big(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp\Big[\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta\right)\begin{Bmatrix}1&,a\delta\geq\mu\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&,a\delta\leq\mu\end{Bmatrix}\Big]\Big)

for all
\chi_{k}\in\left\{0,-1\right\}
, where
W_{0}
and
W_{-1}
are the two real branches of the Lambert W function. The ‘no solution’ case corresponds precisely to
W_{0}
and
W_{-1}
not being defined for this input. Substituting these
x_{k}
back into (
5.7
) gives the condition (
3.10
) as required.

(b) This follows immediately from the large deviation principle in Theorem
2.4
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.12
.
We know that the global minimiser
\xi
of
F_{\mu}
will equal

\xi_{k}=\xi_{k}(\delta,\chi)=-\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}W_{\chi_{k}}\Big(-b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp\Big[\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta\right)\,,\quad k\in\mathbb{N},\begin{Bmatrix}1&,a\delta\geq\mu\\
-\frac{b}{a-b}&,a\delta\leq\mu\end{Bmatrix}\Big]\Big)

(5.8)

for a choice of
(\delta,\chi)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{0,-1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
that satisfies

\delta=g^{\chi}(\delta):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k\xi_{k}(\delta,\chi)\,.

(5.9)

We first observe that the argument of the Lambert function in (
5.8
) is decreasing in
k
and in
\delta\geq\mu/a
. Therefore, to ensure that the function
g^{\chi}
is finite and well-defined (i.e., series converges) the only admissible sequences
\chi
as such that only finitely many indices are
-1
with all remaining ones pointing to the zero branch of the Lambert function. Furthermore, note that
W_{-}(x)\geq\log(-x)
for
x\in[-{\rm e}^{-1},0)
. So if
\chi_{k}=-1
, then for
\delta>\mu/a
we have

g^{\chi}(\delta)>\frac{(a\delta-\mu)}{b}k-\frac{1}{b\beta}\log\big(b\beta k^{2}q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\big)\,.

Therefore there exists
\mu_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that for
\mu<\mu_{1}
the equation
\delta=g^{\chi}(\delta)
has no solution for any of these
\chi
with a finite number of negative branches. Furthermore, if
\chi\equiv 0
, then
g^{\chi}
is strictly decreasing and continuous for sufficiently small values of
\mu
. Note that
g^{\chi}
in this case is decreasing for any
\delta>\mu/a
with maximum at
\mu/a
. Hence for sufficiently small values of
\mu<\mu_{-}=\mu_{-}(d,\beta,\alpha,a,b)
there is a unique solution for
\delta=g^{\chi}(\delta)
(
5.9
).

For the large positive
\mu
case, we need to reduce the admissible number of values of
(\delta,\chi)
we are investigating. We do this separately for the two regimes of
\delta
,
\delta<\mu/a
and
\delta>\mu/a
. First suppose
\delta<\mu/a
and there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that we have a negative branch
\chi_{k}=-1
. Then we compute the second derivative

\frac{\partial^{2}F_{\mu}}{\partial x_{k}^{2}}\Big|_{x=\xi(\delta,\chi)}=\frac{1}{\xi(\delta,\chi)}-b\beta k^{2}\big(1+\frac{a}{a-b}\big)<-\frac{ab}{(a-b)}\beta k^{2}\,,

(5.10)

where the inequality holds because
\chi_{k}=-1
implies that
\xi_{k}>\frac{1}{\beta\beta k^{2}}
. Since this
1
-dimensional Hessian is negative, this
\xi(\delta,\chi)
cannot be a local minimum and therefore not a global minimum. Now suppose that
\delta>\mu/a
and there exist
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
with
k_{1}\not=k_{2}
such that
\chi_{k_{1}}=\chi_{k_{2}}=-1
. We then consider the two-dimensional Hessian in the
k_{1}
and the
k_{2}
coordinate directions. We find

\det\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}F_{\mu}}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}\Big|_{x=\xi(\delta,\chi)}\Big)_{i,j\in\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}=\big(\frac{1}{\xi_{k_{1}}}+(a-b)\beta k_{1}^{2}\big)\big(\frac{1}{\xi_{k_{2}}}+(a-b)\beta k^{2}\big)-a^{2}\beta^{2}k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}<0\,,

where the inequality follows from
\xi_{k}>\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}
if
\chi_{k}=-1
like before. Since this two-dimensional Hessian has a negative eigenvalue, this
\xi(\delta,\chi)
cannot be a local minimum and therefore not a global minimum. A similar analysis shows this for a finite number of distinct negative branches. In summary, we are left with solution of
\delta=g^{\chi}(\delta)
(
5.9
) for which
\chi\equiv 0
or there exists at most a single negative branch
k
with
\chi_{k}=-1
and
\delta\geq\mu/a
. In order to compare the remaining different candidates for minimiser, we calculate

F_{\mu}(\xi)=-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\xi_{k}}{\beta}+\frac{b}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}k^{2}\xi_{k}^{2}-\frac{a}{2}\delta^{2}-\frac{~(\mu-a\delta)_{+}^{2}}{2(a-b)}\,.

Here we used (
5.9
) and the property of the Lambert
W
function that
W_{\chi_{k}}(x)\exp(W_{\chi_{k}}(x))=x
. We then approximate the remaining candidates for large values of
\mu
. For
\mu\gg 1
,
g^{0}(\delta)=\delta
is solved only by a
\delta\ll 1
. We refer to this
\chi\equiv 0
solution as
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}
. Then

F_{\mu}(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})})=-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2(a-b)}+o(1)\,,

as
\mu\to\infty
. For the remaining competitors for minimiser, we let
\xi^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}}
refer to any solution corresponding to
\chi_{k}=-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{k=K\},k\in\mathbb{N}
. We are solely concerned with
\delta\geq\mu/a
for these cases. Hence
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{K}=\frac{1}{K}(\delta+O(1))
, and
\xi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}<\frac{1}{b\beta k^{2}}
otherwise. This means

F_{\mu}(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})})=-\frac{\delta}{K}-\frac{(a-b)}{2}\delta^{2}+O(1)\,,

for
\mu\to\infty
.

If we investigate the difference
F_{\mu}(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})})-F_{\mu}(\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})})
, we find that
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}
is preferred if

\delta\gg\frac{1}{\beta K(a-b)}\big(-1+\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}\mu^{2}K^{2}}\big)<\frac{\mu}{(a-b)}-\frac{1}{\beta K(a-b)}\,.

(5.11)

Conversely,
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}
is preferred if

\delta\ll\frac{\mu}{(a-b)}-\frac{1}{\beta K(a-b)}\,.

In particular, this means that we need
a\delta\gg\mu
if
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}
is to stand a chance. Now for
a\delta\gg\mu
, and
\chi_{k}=-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{k=K\}
we have

g^{\chi}(\delta)=\frac{a\delta-\mu}{a}+\frac{1}{b\beta K}\log(a\delta-\mu)+O(1)\,.

For this calculation we use that

W_{-1}(x)=\log(-x)-\log(-\log(-x))+o(1)\quad\mbox{ as }x\uparrow 0\,.

Using this approximation with
\delta-g^{\chi}(\delta)
tells us that

\delta=\frac{\mu}{(a-b)}-\frac{1}{\beta K(a-b)}\log(a\delta-\mu)+O(1)\,,

and therefore
\xi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({0}})}
is always preferred for large values of
\mu\geq\mu^{+}(d,\beta,\alpha,a,b)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.14
.
We split the proof into three parts. In part I we discuss general solutions for selections of
\chi
. In Step II we show that for the parameter regime given in the theorem one can rule out all
\chi\not=0
solutions. In the final step III we prove the existence of the critical
\mu^{*}
with two simultaneous minimiser (zeros) for the
\chi=0
solution.

Step I:
Note that for
d\geq 3
, the arguments for the Lambert W functions are strictly increasing in the summation index
k
, approaching
0
. This means that since the difference
W_{0}\left(x\right)-W_{-1}\left(x\right)\geq 0
is strictly increasing in
x
and equals
0
if and only if
x=-{\rm e}^{-1}
, we only need to consider finitely many
\chi
for a given
\mu
(all of which are eventually
0
). Now since any non-convexity in
g^{\chi}
can only arrive via the finitely many
\chi_{k}=-1
terms, solutions to
\delta=g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right)
are locally finite in
\mathbb{R}
. To complement this, note that
\lim_{\delta\to+\infty}g^{0}\left(\delta\right)=0
whilst for
\chi\neq 0
we have
g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right)\gg\delta
. Hence we only need to consider a finite range of
\delta
, and therefore for a given
\mu
there are only finitely many solutions for
\delta
.

Because
g^{\chi}
is continuous for each
\chi
, we can collect solutions uniquely and maximally into continuous paths
\xi^{j}\left(\mu\right)
defined on closed (possibly infinite) intervals
I^{j}
with non-empty interior. We allow families to overlap at endpoints of these intervals. Because we are only considering
\mu\leq\mu_{\rm p}
and there are only finitely many solutions for each
\mu
, we will only have finitely many families being relevant to our discussion.

For each of these families, we will denote

D^{j}\left(\mu\right):=D\left(\xi^{j}\left(\mu\right)\right),\qquad P^{j}\left(\mu\right):=-\left(I_{\alpha}+H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}\right)\left(\xi^{j}\left(\mu\right)\right),

defined on the interval
I^{j}
. From Proposition
3.11
we know that

p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha,\mu\right)=-\inf_{y\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\left\{I_{\alpha}(y)+H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}(y)\right\}=\max_{j}P^{j}\left(\mu\right).

Therefore for each
\mu
, there exists a
J
such that
p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\mu\right)=P^{J}\left(\mu\right)
.

From the continuity of
g^{\chi}
we know that all
D^{j}
are continuous on their
I^{j}
. Then Lemma
5.1
tells us that each
P^{j}
is differentiable on the interior
\mathrm{Int}(I^{j})
, with derivative

\frac{{\rm d}P^{j}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D^{j}+\frac{\left(\mu-aD^{j}\right)_{+}}{a-b}.

Continuity of this derivative follows from the continuity of
D^{j}
.

Step II:
We show that for the following values of
b
and
\mu
, i.e.,

b<\min\big\{a,\frac{1}{\beta q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}{\rm e}^{-\mu_{\rm p}/a}\big\}\quad\mbox{ and }\;\mu<\mu_{\rm p}\,,

we do not need to consider the
\chi\not=0
solutions of
\delta=g^{\chi}(\delta)
. Note that if
\chi\not=0
, then
g^{\chi}(\delta)>g^{\chi^{\scriptscriptstyle{({1}})}}(\delta)
, where
\chi_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({1}})}=-{\mathchoice{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.0mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-4.5mu\mathrm{l}}{1\mskip-5.0mu\mathrm{l}}}\{k=1\}
. By using
W_{-1}(x)\leq\log(-x)
, we find that if
\chi\not=0
we have

g^{\chi}(\delta)>\frac{a}{b}\delta-\frac{\mu}{b}-\frac{1}{\beta}\log(\beta q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})})+\frac{1}{\beta}\log(1/b)\,.

Therefore we have no solutions if

b<\frac{1}{\beta q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\exp\big(\beta\big(\frac{(a-b)}{b}\delta-\frac{\mu}{a}\big)\big)\,.

The right hand side of the last inequality is bounded by
\frac{1}{\beta q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}{\rm e}^{-\beta\mu/a}
, which is in turn bounded below by
\frac{1}{\beta q_{1}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}{\rm e}^{-\beta\mu_{\rm p}/a}
for the range of
\mu
we are considering. Since - by our assumptions -
b
is smaller than this bound, we can ignore
\chi
other than
\chi=0
.

\mu
0
D^{0,1,2}
\mu_{\rm p}
\mu_{\rm tang}
\frac{\mu_{\rm p}}{a}
D^{0}
D^{1}
D^{2}

Figure 5
.
Sketch of the total particle density of the three
\chi=0
solutions for
d=3,4
.

Step III:
Let us now consider the
\chi=0
solutions. Since
g^{0}
is convex when restricted to
\delta\leq\frac{\mu}{a}
,
\frac{{\rm d}g^{\chi}}{{\rm d}\delta}\to+\infty
as
\delta\uparrow\frac{\mu}{a}
, and
g^{0}
is decreasing for
\delta\geq\frac{\mu}{a}
, there exists
\mu_{\rm tang}<\mu_{\rm p}
such that this branch has multiple solutions if and only if
\mu\in\left[\mu_{\rm tang},\mu_{\rm p}\right]
. Let us label these
\xi^{0}
,
\xi^{1}
, and
\xi^{2}
such that
D^{0}>D^{1}\geq D^{2}
. Note that
I^{0}=\left(-\infty,\mu_{\rm p}\right]
,
I^{1}=\left[\mu_{\rm tang},\mu_{\rm p}\right]
, and
I^{2}=\left[\mu_{\rm tang},+\infty\right)
. For a visualisation of these solutions, see Figure
5
.

Since
\xi^{0}\left(\mu_{\rm p}\right)=\xi^{1}\left(\mu_{\rm p}\right)
and
\xi^{1}\left(\mu_{\rm tang}\right)=\xi^{2}\left(\mu_{\rm tang}\right)
, we have
P^{0}\left(\mu_{\rm p}\right)=P^{1}\left(\mu_{\rm p}\right)
and
P^{1}\left(\mu_{\rm tang}\right)=P^{2}\left(\mu_{\rm tang}\right)
. Because
D^{0}\geq\frac{\mu}{a}
and
D^{1,2}\leq\frac{\mu}{a}
, we have

\frac{{\rm d}P^{0}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D^{0},\qquad\frac{{\rm d}P^{1}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D^{1}\right)<\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D^{2}\right)=\frac{{\rm d}P^{2}}{{\rm d}\mu},

on
\left(\mu_{\rm tang},\mu_{\rm p}\right)
. Together these mean that
P^{2}\left(\mu_{\rm p}\right)>P^{1}\left(\mu_{\rm p}\right)=P^{0}\left(\mu_{\rm p}\right)
. Now extending our attention to all
\xi^{j}
defined on some part of
\left(-\infty,\mu_{\rm p}\right]
, we define

M:=\left\{\mu\leq\mu_{\rm p}:\exists\,j\text{ such that }P^{j}\left(\mu\right)>P^{0}\left(\mu\right)\right\}\,.

We have just shown that
M\neq\varnothing
, so
\mu^{*}:=\inf M\in(-\infty,\mu_{\rm p}]
. Since
P^{2}
and
P^{0}
are continuous,
\mu^{*}<\mu_{\rm p}
. If
\mu^{*}\in M
, then
\max_{j}P^{j}\left(\mu\right)
is discontinuous at
\mu=\mu^{*}
. In this case we are done. If otherwise
\mu^{*}\notin M
, then since the
P^{j}
are each continuous,
\exists\,J
and
\epsilon>0
such that
P^{J}\left(\mu\right)>P^{0}\left(\mu\right)
for
\mu\in\left(\mu^{*},\mu^{*}+\epsilon\right)
. Now we have to show that the derivatives of
P^{0}
and
P^{J}
necessarily have different limits as we take
\mu\to\mu^{*}
from their respective sides. First note that

\lim_{\mu\uparrow\mu^{*}}\frac{{\rm d}P^{0}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D^{0}\left(\mu^{*}\right)\,.

Note that
g^{\chi}\left(\delta\right)\geq g^{0}\left(\delta\right)
with equality only if
\chi=0
or if we have both
\beta=\beta^{*}
and
\delta=\frac{\mu}{a}
. Therefore
D^{J}\neq\frac{\mu}{a}
and

\displaystyle D^{J}<\frac{\mu}{a}

\displaystyle\implies D^{J}\in\left[D^{2},D^{1}\right]\,,

\displaystyle D^{J}>\frac{\mu}{a}

\displaystyle\implies D^{J}>D^{0}\,.

This last inequality is strict because equality could only occur at
\mu=\mu_{\rm p}
, but
\mu^{*}<\mu_{\rm p}
. If
D^{J}>\frac{\mu}{a}
, then

\lim_{\mu\downarrow\mu^{*}}\frac{{\rm d}P^{J}}{{\rm d}\mu}=D^{J}\left(\mu^{*}\right)>D^{0}\left(\mu^{*}\right)=\lim_{\mu\uparrow\mu^{*}}\frac{{\rm d}P^{0}}{{\rm d}\mu}

and we are done. From the symmetry of
g^{0}
about
\delta=\frac{\mu}{a}
and from
g^{0}
being decreasing for
\delta\geq\frac{\mu}{a}
, we have

D^{0}-\frac{\mu}{a}<\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{a}-D^{1}\right)

(5.12)

for
\mu\in\left[\mu_{\rm tang},\mu_{\rm p}\right)
, see Figure
6
. To see that, note that from the definition of
g^{0}(d)
, there is a symmetry to the function: the function to the right of
\mu/a
is a scaled and reflected version of the function to the left. In Figure
6
, this means that the distance from the leftmost intersection (the
D^{1}
intersection) to
\mu/a
gives you the distance from
\mu/a
to the imagined rightmost point. Since
g^{0}
is decreasing to the right of
\mu/a
, this imagined point is to the right of the
D^{2}
intersection, giving the inequality. This implies that if
D^{J}<\frac{\mu}{a}
,

\frac{{\rm d}P^{J}}{{\rm d}\mu}=\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D^{J}\right)\geq\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{b}-D^{1}\right)>D^{0}=\frac{{\rm d}P^{0}}{{\rm d}\mu}.

Taking the limit to
\mu^{*}
gives our result.

\frac{\mu}{a}-D^{1}
D^{0}-\frac{\mu}{a}
\frac{b}{a-b}\left(\frac{\mu}{a}-D^{1}\right)

.
Sketch of inequality (
5.12
).
