Proof of Theorem
3.16
.
Let us begin with the
\alpha<0
case. For
s\leq-\alpha
, fixed
N
, and fixed
K
, define

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right):=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\left[\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta\left(D-D_{K}\right)\right)\right].

Then

\frac{{\rm d}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}}{{\rm d}s}\Big|_{s=0}=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\big[D-D_{K}\big]\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta\left(\beta,\alpha\right)=\lim_{K\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{{\rm d}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}}{{\rm d}s}\Big|_{s=0}.

Since
g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}
are all convex in
s
, we will use Griffith’s Lemma to get the point-wise limit of the derivative from the derivative of the point-wise limit.
To calculate the point-wise limit of
g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}
, we first rewrite
g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}
as

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha+s}}\left[\exp\left(-\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta D_{K}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\frac{Z_{N}\left(\beta,\alpha+s\right)}{Z_{N}\left(\beta,\alpha\right)}.

We use Varadhan’s Lemma with the tilt
\Phi=-s\beta D_{K}
. This
\Phi
is continuous, but we need to pay attention to the boundedness conditions. We need to show that

\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha+s}}\big[{\rm e}^{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|\Phi}\mathds{1}\left\{\Phi\geq M\right\}\big]=-\infty.

(5.13)

For
0\leq s\leq-\alpha
, we have
\Phi\leq 0
almost surely, so (
5.13
) holds trivially. For
s<0
we have to work a little harder. Since
\Phi
is continuous, the set
\left\{\Phi=m\right\}
is closed (and measurable). Hence the upper bound of the LDP gives

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\nu_{N,\alpha+s}\left(\Phi=m\right)\leq-\inf_{y\colon\Phi=m}\{I_{\alpha+s}(y)\}\leq\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha+s}})}-\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}+\left(\alpha+s\right)\big(\frac{m}{\lvert s\rvert\beta}\big).

This means that for sufficiently large
N
there exists a
m
and
N
independent constant
C>\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha+s}})}-\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}
, such that

{\rm e}^{m\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\nu_{N,\alpha+s}\left(\Phi=m\right)\leq\exp\big(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\big[C+\frac{\alpha}{\lvert s\rvert}m\big]\big)\,.

Since
\alpha<0
, we have sufficiently fast decay in
m
to prove that (
5.13
) holds even for
s<0
, and Varadhan gives us

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=-\inf_{y\in\ell_{1}}\left\{I_{\alpha+s}(y)+sD_{K}(y)\right\}+p\left(\beta,\alpha+s\right)-p\left(\beta,\alpha\right),\qquad\forall s\leq-\alpha.

In the style of Lemma
B.3
, we can find that this infimum is achieved at
\xi=\xi(s)\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
, where

\xi_{k}=\begin{cases}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}&,k=1,\ldots,K\,,\\
q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha+s}})}}&,k>K\,.\end{cases}

Hence

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum^{\infty}_{k=K+1}\left(q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha+s}})}}-q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right),\qquad\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\to\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}=\sum^{\infty}_{k=K+1}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\,.

Finally the sum vanishes as
K\to\infty
.

For the case
\alpha=0
with
d=1,2
, we take a more direct approach. It is clear from our construction that the point-wise limit
\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\left[kx_{k}\right]=kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}
. Then for all
M\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\Big[\sum^{\infty}_{k=K+1}kx_{k}\Big]\geq\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\Big[\sum^{M}_{k=K+1}kx_{k}\Big]=\sum^{M}_{k=K+1}kq_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\,.

Since this lower bound diverges as
M\to\infty
if
\alpha=0
and
d=1,2
, we have our result for this case.

For
\alpha=0
and
d\geq 3
we use direct methods similar to the
\alpha>0
case to get the required results.
For the CMF-model we follow the same steps as above. For
s\leq-\alpha
, fixed
N
, and fixed
K
, define

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right):=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}}\left[\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta\left(D-D_{K}\right)\right)\right].

Then by rearranging terms and applying Varadhan, we find that for
s\leq-\alpha
,

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=-\inf_{\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\big\{\frac{1}{\beta}I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha+s}+sD_{K}\big\}+p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha+s\right)-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{855497}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647441}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle CMF$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{647440}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle CMF$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha\right),\qquad\forall s\leq-\alpha\,.

In the style of Lemma
3.1
, we can find that this infimum is achieved at
\xi\left(s\right)\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
, where

\xi_{k}=\begin{cases}\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}&,k\leq K,\\
\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha+s}})}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha+s}})}}q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha+s}})}}&,k>K.\end{cases}

Substituting this into the infimum, and then taking the derivative gives us

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\to\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}=\frac{W_{0}\left(a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\right)}{a\beta\bar{q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\sum^{\infty}_{k=K+1}kq^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}_{k}.

Finally the sum vanishes as
K\to\infty
, and Griffith’s Lemma gives us the result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.17
.
Our proof begins similarly to that of Theorem
3.16
. For
\mu,s\in\mathbb{R}
, fixed
N
, and fixed
K
, define

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha,\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}\left[\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta\left(D-D_{K}\right)\right)\right].

We once again rearrange terms to get

g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)=\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha,\mu+s}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}\left[\exp\left(-\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|s\beta D_{K}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{\beta\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\frac{Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N}\left(\beta,\alpha,\mu+s\right)}{Z^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N}\left(\beta,\alpha,\mu\right)}.

Then we want to use Varadhan’s Lemma with our LDP for the PMF measure and the tilt
\phi=-s\beta D_{K}
. This
\phi
is continuous, but we need to pay attention to the boundedness conditions. We will show

\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha,\mu+s}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}}\Big[{\rm e}^{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|\phi}\mathds{1}\left\{\phi\geq M\right\}\Big]=-\infty.

(5.14)

For
s\geq 0
, we have
\phi\leq 0
almost surely, so (
5.14
) holds trivially. For
s<0
we have to work a little harder. Our LDP for the PMF model gives us a bound on the probability of this set:

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\nu^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N,\alpha,\mu+s}\left(\phi=m\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\inf_{\phi=m}I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu+s}

\displaystyle\frac{m}{\left|s\right|\beta}\geq\frac{\mu+s}{a}\implies\qquad

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\ell_{1}}\left\{I_{\alpha}+H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+s,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}\right\}+\beta(\mu+s)\left(\frac{m}{\lvert s\rvert\beta}\right)-\frac{a\beta}{2}\left(\frac{m}{\lvert s\rvert\beta}\right)^{2}.

This means that given
m\geq\left|s\right|\beta\frac{\mu+s}{a}
, then for sufficiently large
N
there exists a
m
and
N
independent constant
C>\inf_{\ell_{1}}\left\{I_{0}+\beta H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu+s,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}\right\}
, such that

{\rm e}^{m\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\nu^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{N,\alpha,\mu+s}\left(\phi=m\right)\leq\exp\Big(\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\left[C+\frac{\mu}{\lvert s\rvert m}-\frac{a}{2\beta\lvert s\rvert^{2}}m^{2}\right]\Big)\,.

The very fast decay with
m
proves that (
5.14
) holds even for
s<0
, and Varadhan gives us

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}(s)=-\inf_{\ell_{1}}\left\{I^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\alpha,\mu+s}+sD_{K}\right\}+p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha,\mu+s\right)-p^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}\left(\beta,\alpha,\mu\right),\qquad\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

In the style of Lemma
3.6
, we can find that this infimum is achieved at
\xi\left(s\right)\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
, where

\xi_{k}=q_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp\left(\beta k\left[\left(\mu+s-a\delta^{*}\right)_{-}-s\mathds{1}\left\{k\leq K\right\}\right]\right),\quad k\in\mathbb{N},

and
\delta^{*}\left(s\right)
is given implicitly as follows for the different cases. For
\mu+s\leq 0
and
\mu\leq 0
, we have that

\delta^{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}kq_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp(\beta k(\mu-a\delta^{*}))+\sum_{k>K}kq_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp(\beta k(\mu+s-a\delta^{*}))=:\varrho^{K}(\alpha+\mu+s)\,,

(5.15)

that is,
\delta^{*}<\varrho^{K}(\alpha+\mu+s)
, and for
\mu+s>0
we have two cases as follows.

\delta^{*}=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=1}^{K}kq_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp(\beta k(\mu-a\delta^{*}))+\sum_{k>K}kq_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp(\beta k(\mu+s-a\delta^{*}))&,\mbox{ for }\delta^{*}\geq\frac{\mu+s}{a}\,,\\
\sum_{k=1}^{K}kq_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}\exp(-\beta ks)+\sum_{k>K}kq_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}=:\varrho^{K}(\alpha,s)&,\mbox{ for }\delta^{*}\leq\frac{\mu+s}{a}\,.\end{cases}

(5.16)

Thus

\delta^{*}=\begin{cases}\in(0,\varrho^{K}(\alpha+\mu+s)&,\mbox{ for }\mu+s\leq 0,\mu\leq 0\,,\\
\begin{cases}\in(\frac{\mu+s}{a},\varrho^{K}(\alpha,s))&,\mbox{ for }\mu+s<a\varrho^{K}(\alpha,s)\,,\\
\varrho^{K}(\alpha,s)&,\mbox{ for }\mu+s\geq a\varrho^{K}(\alpha,s)\,.\end{cases}&,\mbox{ for }\mu+s>0\,.\end{cases}

(5.17)

If we denote

\delta^{*}_{K}=\begin{cases}\sum^{K}_{k=1}kq_{k}\exp\left(\beta k\left(\mu-a\delta^{*}\right)\right)&,\mbox{ for }\delta^{*}\geq\frac{\mu+s}{a},\\
\sum^{K}_{k=1}kq_{k}\exp\left(-s\beta k\right)&,\mbox{ for }\delta^{*}\leq\frac{\mu+s}{a},\end{cases}

(5.18)

then Lemma
5.1
tells us that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=\left(\delta^{*}-\delta^{*}_{K}\right)\left(s\right)+\big(\frac{\mu+s}{a}-\varrho^{K}(\alpha,s)\big)_{+}

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(\delta^{*}-\delta^{*}_{K}\right)\left(0\right)&,\mbox{ for }\mu\leq a\varrho^{K}(\alpha,0)\,,\\
\frac{\mu}{a}-\delta^{*}_{K}\left(0\right)&,\mbox{ for }\mu\geq a\varrho^{K}(\alpha,0)\,,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\lim_{K\to\infty}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\Big(\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{({K}})}_{N}\left(s\right)\Big)\Big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mu}{a}-\varrho(\alpha)\right)_{+}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
3.18
.
The proof of (
3.20
) follows very similarly to the corresponding stage of the proof of Theorem
3.17
. Note that the HYL version of (
5.14
) follows because
H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{792963}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585927}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle HYL$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{585928}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle HYL$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}\geq H^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{646131}{167117}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{PMF}$}}}}}}})}_{\mu,a-b,\rm\tiny l.s.c.}
almost surely. The proof of (
3.21
) uses Lemma
5.1
.
∎

Appendices

Appendix A
Bose function

The Bose functions are poly-logarithmic functions defined by

g(n,\alpha):=\operatorname{Li}\,_{n}({\rm e}^{-\alpha})=\frac{1}{\Gamma(n)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n-1}}{{\rm e}^{t+\alpha}-1}\,{\rm d}t=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{-n}{\rm e}^{-\alpha k}\quad\mbox{ for all }n\mbox{ and }\alpha>0,

(A.1)

and also for
\alpha=0
and
n>1
. In the latter case,

g(n,0)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{-n}=\zeta(n),

(A.2)

which is the zeta function of Riemann. The behaviour of the Bose functions about
\alpha=0
is given by

g(n,\alpha)=\begin{cases}\Gamma(1-n)\alpha^{n-1}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\zeta(n-k)\frac{(-\alpha)^{k}}{k!}&,n\not=1,2,3,\ldots,\\[6.45831pt]
\frac{(-\alpha)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\left[-\log\alpha+\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{m}\right]+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k=0}{k\not= n-1}}\zeta(n-k)\frac{(-\alpha)^{k}}{k!}&,n\in\mathbb{N}.\end{cases}

(A.3)

Appendix B
The ideal Bose gas

We review the large deviation principle for the ideal Bose, that is, large deviation principle for the empirical cycles counts. However, we present this well-known result by using rigorous large deviation proofs based on Baldi’s Theorem and exponential tightness. Denote
\nu_{N,\alpha}
the distribution of the empirical cycle count with chemical potential
\alpha\leq 0
.

Theorem B.1

(
Ideal Bose gas
)
.

For
d\in\mathbb{N},\beta>0
, and
\alpha\leq 0
, the sequence
(\nu_{N,\alpha})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviation principle (LDP) on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
with rate
\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert
and rate function

I_{\alpha}\left(x\right)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\frac{x_{k}}{\beta}\Big(\log\frac{x_{k}}{q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}-1\Big)+\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}/\beta\,.

Since each entry in the
\ell_{1}
-valued empirical cycle count is independent and related to a Poisson random variable under the ideal Bose gas model, Theorem
B.1
is proven by applying Baldi’s Lemma.

Remark B.2
.

The condition that
\alpha\leq 0
arises from the
\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}
term. Clearly,
\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}
is finite if and only if
\alpha\leq 0
.
\diamond

Proposition B.3

(
Pressure
)
.

(a)

The rate function for the ideal Bose gas model,
I_{\alpha}
, has a unique zero
\xi\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
given by

\xi_{k}=q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(B.1)

(b)

Let
\beta>0
and
\alpha\leq 0
, then the thermodynamic limit of the pressure

\displaystyle p(\beta,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log{\rm e}^{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\rm bc}})},\alpha}}=\inf_{y\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}\Big\{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{y_{k}}{\beta}\Big(\log\frac{y_{k}}{q_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}-1\Big)\Big\}=\frac{\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}{\beta}

(B.2)

exists.

We denote

p_{\Lambda_{N}}=\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}Z_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\alpha)

the average finite-volume pressure.

Proposition B.4

(
Thermodynamics
)
.

(a)

For
\beta>0
,
\alpha>0
, we define
p(\beta,\alpha)=+\infty
. Then the pressure
p(\beta,\cdot)
is a closed convex function on
\mathbb{R}
.

(b)

For
\beta>0
,
\alpha<0
, the ideal gas pressure
p(\beta,\alpha)
is smooth with respect to
\alpha
. In particular,

\frac{{\rm d}p}{{\rm d}\alpha}=D\left(q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\right).

(c)

For
\beta>0,\alpha<0
, and any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}{\tt E}\big[N_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}\big]=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\alpha}p_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\alpha).

(B.3)

The function
\alpha\mapsto\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\alpha}p_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\alpha)
is increasing on
(-\infty,0)
. It follows that we can give
\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}{\tt E}[N_{\Lambda_{N}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\ell}})}}]
any pre-assigned value
\varrho\in(0,\infty)
by choosing
\alpha=\alpha_{N}(\varrho)\in(-\infty,0)
.

(d)

In the thermodynamic limit
N\to\infty
,

\varrho_{\rm c}:=\lim_{\alpha\uparrow 0}\Big(\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\alpha}p_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\alpha)\Big)=\begin{cases}+\infty&,d=1,2,\\
\frac{1}{\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\zeta\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)&,d\geq 3.\end{cases}

(B.4)

Let
\alpha_{N}(\varrho)
denote the unique root of

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\alpha}\big(p_{\Lambda_{N}}(\beta,\alpha)\big)=\varrho

(B.5)

then
\alpha(\varrho)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\alpha_{N}(\varrho)
exists and is equal to the unique root of

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\alpha}\big(p(\beta,\alpha)\big)=\varrho\quad\mbox{ if }\varrho<\varrho_{\rm c},

(B.6)

and it is equal to zero otherwise.

Proposition B.5

(
Free energy
)
.

For
\varrho>0
, we define the ideal Bose gas free energy as the Legendre-Fenchel transform of the pressure,

\displaystyle f\left(\beta,\varrho\right):=\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{s\varrho-p\left(\beta,s\right)\right\}=\begin{cases}\frac{-1}{\beta\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}g(1+\frac{d}{2},-\beta\gamma)+\varrho\gamma&,\varrho\leq\varrho_{\rm c}\;,\\
\frac{-1}{\beta\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\zeta\left(1+\frac{d}{2}\right)&,\varrho\geq\varrho_{\rm c}\;,\end{cases}

(B.7)

where
\gamma\leq 0
is a solution to

\frac{1}{\left(4\pi\beta\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}}g\big(\frac{d}{2},-\beta\gamma\big)=\varrho,

which exists and is unique for
\varrho\leq\varrho_{\rm c}
.

It is easy to see that
\varrho\mapsto f(\beta,\varrho)
is a decreasing convex function; it is given by

f(\beta,\varrho)=\alpha(\varrho)\varrho-p(\beta,\alpha(\varrho))\quad\mbox{ for }\varrho<\varrho_{\rm c}.

The linear segment in the graph of
f
where
f
is constant and equal to
-p(\beta,0)
for
\varrho\geq\varrho_{\rm c}
, signals a first-order phase-transition at
\alpha=0
. This phase-transition is called
Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC)
.

Proofs for the reference measure (ideal Bose gas)
:

Proof of Theorem
B.1
.
We recall the following theorem for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma B.6

(
Baldi’s Theorem
)
.

Suppose
(\nu_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is an exponentially tight sequence of measures on
\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\Lambda\colon\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\to[0,\infty]
be the limiting cumulant generating function, and suppose that it exists and is finite for every
t\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. If
\Lambda
is Gâteaux differentiable, and lower semicontinuous on
\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, then
(\nu_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies an LDP with rate function

\Lambda^{*}(x)=\sup_{t\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\big\{\langle t,x\rangle-\Lambda(t)\big\},\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}).

(B.8)

We shall now set about establishing that the hypotheses of Baldi’s Theorem are satisfied. We adapt a beautiful proof in a recent study of Bosonic loop measures on graphs given in
[
Dan15
]
.

Lemma B.7

(
Exponential tightness
)
.

For every
\alpha\leq 0
,
\big(\nu_{N,\mu}\big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is an exponentially tight sequence of measures.

Proof.
Suppose there exists an
x=x\left(\gamma\right)\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
such that for all
k\geq 1
,

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\nu_{N,\alpha}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}\geq x_{k}\right)<-2^{-k}\gamma,

where
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}=\big(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is an
\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
-valued random variable with law
\nu_{N,\alpha}
. Also, define the set

K=\left\{y\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\colon\left|y_{k}\right|\leq\left|x_{k}\right|\forall k\geq 1\right\},\qquad x\in\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R}).

To show compactness of
K
it is easy to see that
K
is bounded and closed. Boundedness follows from
\lVert y\rVert_{\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\lVert x\rVert_{\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}
for all
y\in K
. Suppose that
K
is not closed, that is, there exists a sequence
y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}\in K
with limit
y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}\to y\notin K
as
n\to\infty
. Suppose that
\lvert y_{k}\rvert>\lvert x_{k}\rvert
for
y\notin K
and some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Choose
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}(\lvert y_{k}\rvert-\lvert x_{k}\rvert)
, then, for
n
sufficiently large,

\lvert y_{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}-y_{k}\rvert\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}_{j}-y_{j}\rvert<\frac{1}{2}(\lvert y_{k}\rvert-\lvert x_{k}\rvert),

which implies that

\lvert y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}_{k}\rvert>\lvert y_{k}\rvert-\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}(\lvert y_{k}\rvert+\lvert x_{k}\rvert)>\lvert x_{k}\rvert,

contradicting
y^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({n}})}}\in K
. Hence,
K
is closed. It remains to show that
K
is totally bounded. From that, we shall find a finite cover of
\varepsilon
-open balls for
K
. Pick
\varepsilon>0
, and choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{k>N}\lvert x_{k}\rvert<\varepsilon/2
, and define the so-called cut-off sequences
\widetilde{K}=\{y\in K\colon y_{k}=0,k>N\}
. Clearly,
\widetilde{K}
is isomorphic to the totally bounded set

[-\lvert x_{1}\rvert,\lvert x_{1}\rvert]\times\cdots[-\lvert x_{N}\rvert,\lvert x_{N}\rvert]\subset\mathbb{R}^{N},

and thus it is itself totally bounded. There exist
w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({1}})}},\ldots,w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({M}})}}\in\widetilde{K}
such that

\widetilde{K}\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}B(w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}},\frac{\varepsilon}{2}).

For any
y\in
denote
\widetilde{y}\in\widetilde{K}
the sequences which agrees with
y
on the first
N
terms, and choose
w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}
such that
\widetilde{y}\in B(w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}},\frac{\varepsilon}{2})
. Then,

\lVert y-w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}\rVert_{\ell_{1}(\mathbb{R})}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\lvert\widetilde{y}_{k}-w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}}_{k}\rvert+\sum_{k>N}\lvert y_{k}\rvert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Thus,
K\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}B(w^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({i}})}},\frac{\varepsilon}{2})
, and we conclude with the compactness of
K
.

Now, since the
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}
are independent, we have

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\log\nu_{N,\alpha}\left(K^{\rm c}\right)=\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\log\nu_{N,\alpha}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}>x_{k}\right)<-\gamma,

and conclude with the statement in the lemma.
All that remains now is to find such a sequence
x
. We consider each
x_{k}
in turn. For all constants
c\geq 0
, and
\tau>0
, we have the Chernoff bound

\displaystyle\nu_{N,\alpha}\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}>c\right)

\displaystyle=\nu_{N,\alpha}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{\tau}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}>{\rm e}^{\tau c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\rm e}^{-\tau c}{\tt E}\left[{\rm e}^{\frac{\tau}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}{\mathcal{N}}_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{-\tau c}\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{\tau}{\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|}}-1\right)\right).

Differentiating this bound with respect to
\tau
gives us that the minimum occurs at
\tau^{*}=\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\log\frac{c}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}
. If
c>0
, then
\tau^{*}>0
for sufficiently large
N
. This means that we can optimise this form of bound as

\nu_{N,\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}>c)\leq\Big(\frac{c}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}\Big)^{-\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|c}\exp\left(\left|\Lambda_{N}\right|\left(c-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}\right)\right).

Taking
N\rightarrow\infty
then gives us

\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\nu_{N,\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}>c)\leq c-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}-c\log\frac{c}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}.

Now note that on
c>0
, the maps

c\mapsto c-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}-c\log\frac{c}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}+2^{-k}\gamma,\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

are differentiable, strictly decreasing, and have at most a unique zero
c^{*}_{k}
. If there does not exist such a zero, then the map is strictly negative, and it will suffice in what follows to set
c^{*}_{k}=0
. Since our maps are strictly negative for
c>c^{*}_{k}
, we only need to find a sequence
x
such that
x_{k}>c^{*}_{k}
for all
k
. Now we only need to find such an
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
.

Consider
x_{k}=c^{*}_{k}+2^{-k}
. Therefore
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
if and only if
c^{*}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
. If we defined
c^{*}_{k}
as a zero, then
c^{*}_{k}
solves

c^{*}_{k}\Big(1-\log\Big(\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}\Big)\Big)=q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}+2^{-k}\gamma\,.

Otherwise,
c^{*}_{k}=0
and

c^{*}_{k}\Big(1-\log\Big(\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}\Big)\Big)=0.

So noting that
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}>0
and
\gamma>0
and that the sum of
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}
converges give us

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c^{*}_{k}\Big(1-\log\Big(\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}\Big)\Big)\leq\gamma+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}<\infty.

Suppose, for contradiction, that
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c^{*}_{k}=\infty
. Then, in order for the left hand side of the above inequality to converge, we require
1-\log\Big(\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}\Big)\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Consequently, there exists a
K\geq 1
such that for
k\geq K
,
\frac{c^{*}_{k}}{q^{\star}_{k}}<3
, and hence

\sum_{k\geq K}c^{*}_{k}\leq 3\sum_{k\geq K}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}\leq 3\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}<\infty\,.

We have a contradiction, and
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}c^{*}_{k}<\infty
as required.∎

Lemma B.8
.

The limit cumulant generating function exists and is given by

\displaystyle\Lambda\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\Big[\exp\Big(\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\langle t,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}\rangle\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}{\beta}\left({\rm e}^{\beta t_{k}}-1\right)<\infty\,,\qquad t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\,.

Moreover,
\Lambda
is Gâteaux differentiable, lower semicontinuous, and strictly convex.

Proof.

First, let us evaluate the logarithmic moment generating function. Recall, that our reference process is a independent superposition of countably many independent marked Poisson point processes. Denote the marginal law of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}
by
\nu^{(k)}_{N}
, then we have,

\displaystyle\Lambda\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\Big[\exp\Big(\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert\langle t,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{N}\rangle\Big)\Big]=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\log\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{N,\alpha}}\left[\exp\left(\beta\lvert\Lambda_{N}\rvert t_{k}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({k}})}}_{N}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}{\beta}\left({\rm e}^{\beta t_{k}}-1\right).

To see that
\Lambda\left(t\right)
is finite, note that
t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
implies that
T:=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}t_{j}
is finite. Hence

\Lambda\left(t\right)\leq\left({\rm e}^{T}-1\right)\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}<\infty.

To confirm Gâteaux differentiability, let
t,s\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and consider

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\epsilon}\Lambda\left(t+\epsilon s\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}{\beta}\,s_{k}{\rm e}^{\beta(t_{k}+\epsilon s_{k})}.

This sum is finite because
t
and
s
are bounded above and
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
for
\alpha\leq 0
. In particular, the derivative is defined at
\epsilon=0
, and hence
\Lambda
is Gâteaux differentiable.

Lower semicontinuity is an immediate consequence of Fatou’s Lemma. For any sequence
t^{(n)}\rightarrow t
in
\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
,

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda\big(t^{(n)}\big)=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}{\beta}\left({\rm e}^{\beta t^{(n)}_{k}}-1\right)\geq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}{\beta}\left({\rm e}^{\beta t_{k}}-1\right)=\Lambda\left(t\right).

To show strict convexity, consider distinct
t,s\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
and
\lambda\in\left[0,1\right]
. Then

\displaystyle\Lambda\left(\lambda s+\left(1-\lambda\right)t\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\mu}})}}_{k}}{\beta}\left({\rm e}^{\beta(\lambda s_{k}+\left(1-\lambda\right)t_{k})}-1\right)

\displaystyle<\lambda\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}{\beta}{\rm e}^{\beta s_{k}}+\left(1-\lambda\right)\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}{\beta}{\rm e}^{\beta t_{k}}-\frac{\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}}{\beta}

\displaystyle=\lambda\Lambda\left(s\right)+\left(1-\lambda\right)\Lambda\left(t\right),

where the inequality follows from the strict convexity of the exponential function.
∎

Remark B.9
.

If we do not have
\alpha\leq 0
, then we do not have
\Lambda\left(t\right)<\infty
for all
t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
. To see this, let
t
be a constant sequence
t_{k}=C>0
. Then
\Lambda\left(t\right)=C\bar{q}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}=\infty
unless
\alpha\leq 0
.
\diamond

Lemma B.10
.

For all
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, we have

\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right):=\sup_{t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}\left\{\left\langle t,x\right\rangle-\Lambda\left(t\right)\right\}=I_{\alpha}(x)\,.

Proof.

Let
g_{x}\left(t\right)
denote the functional we wish to maximise in the definition of
\Lambda^{*}
, so

g_{x}\left(t\right)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}\big[x_{k}t_{k}+\frac{1}{\beta}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}\big(1-{\rm e}^{\beta t_{k}}\big)\big].

First let us consider
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\setminus\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
. Hence there exists an index
k^{\prime}
such that
x_{k^{\prime}}<0
. Now let
t^{(T)}=-T\delta_{k^{\prime}}\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
. Therefore

\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)\geq g_{x}\big(t^{(T)}\big)=-Tx_{k^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{\beta}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k^{\prime}}\big(1-{\rm e}^{-\beta T}\big)\xrightarrow{T\rightarrow\infty}+\infty.

This means
\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)=+\infty=I_{\alpha}\left(x\right)
for all
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\setminus\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
.

To show the required inequality on
\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, let us now search for critical points of
g_{x}
. Taking the Gâteaux derivative of
g_{x}
gives us

{\rm d}g_{x}\left(t;s\right)=\sum^{\infty}_{k=1}s_{k}\left(x_{k}-q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}{\rm e}^{\beta t_{k}}\right),\qquad\forall t,s\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right).

Now
t
is a critical point if and only if
{\rm d}g_{x}\left(t;s\right)=0

\forall s\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
. This means that we want to investigate the sequence
\tilde{t}_{k}=\frac{1}{\beta}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}
. If
\tilde{t}\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
, then this gives us the supremum, and a simple substitution tells us that
\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)=I_{\alpha}\left(x\right)
for such
x
. Unfortunately, this is not necessarily the case.

Nevertheless, these critical points will give us the supremum over all sequences in
\left(\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{-\infty\right\}\right)^{\mathbb{N}}
. Since
\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\subset\left(\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{-\infty\right\}\right)^{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)=\sup_{t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}g_{x}\left(t\right)\leq\sup_{t\in\left(\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{-\infty\right\}\right)^{\mathbb{N}}}g_{x}\left(t\right)=I_{\alpha}\left(x\right).

(B.9)

To find the reverse inequality, let us consider

t^{(K)}_{k}=\begin{cases}\mathds{1}\left\{k\leq K\right\}\beta^{-1}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}&,x_{k}\neq 0,\\
-K\mathds{1}\left\{k\leq K\right\}&,x_{k}=0.\end{cases}

Since
t^{(K)}
truncates, it is clearly in
\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
for all
K
. Now let us substitute it into
g_{x}
.

\displaystyle g_{x}\left(t^{(K)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\leq K:x_{k}\neq 0}\frac{1}{\beta}\Big(x_{k}\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}-x_{k}+q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}\Big)+\sum_{k\leq K:x_{k}=0}\frac{1}{\beta}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}\left(1-{\rm e}^{-\beta K}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum^{K}_{k=1}\frac{1}{\beta}\Big(x_{k}\Big(\log\frac{x_{k}}{q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}}-1\Big)+q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}\Big]-{\rm e}^{-\beta K}\sum_{k\leq K\colon x_{k}=0}q^{{\scriptscriptstyle{({\alpha}})}}_{k}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{K\rightarrow\infty}I_{\alpha}\left(x\right).

This sequence
\left(t^{(K)}\right)_{K\in\mathbb{N}}
shows that for
x\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
,

\Lambda^{*}\left(x\right)=\sup_{t\in\ell_{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}g_{x}\left(t\right)\geq I_{\alpha}\left(x\right),

as required.
∎

With Baldi’s Theorem the proof of Theorem
B.1
is complete.

∎

Proof of Proposition
B.3
.
(a) To find the zeroes of the ideal gas rate function, first let us find the critical points by setting the Gâteaux derivative of the function to zero. That is, we find the set of points
\tilde{x}\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\right)
such that

{\rm d}I_{\alpha}\left(\tilde{x};y\right)=0\quad\forall y\in\ell_{1}\left(\mathbb{R}\right).

This yields a single equation for each element of the sequence
\tilde{x}
. This set of equations has the unique solution
\tilde{x}=\xi
given by
\xi_{k}=q_{k}{\rm e}^{\beta\alpha k}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since the rate function
I_{\alpha}
is strictly convex where it is finite, this critical point is the unique global minimiser.

(b) The existence of the thermodynamic limit and the explicit function follows from the large deviation rate function
I_{\alpha}
.

∎

Proof of Proposition
B.4
.
(a) Clearly,
p(\beta,0)=\frac{1}{\beta(4\pi\beta)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{1+d/2}}<\infty
for all
d\geq 1
. Convexity follows from properties of the Bose functions,
g(1+\frac{d}{2},-\beta\mu)
, see (
A.1
) in Appendix
A
.

(b) This follows from the Bose functions,
g(n,x)
, being differentiable for
x>0
, and

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}x}g(n,x)=-g(n-1,x),\qquad\forall x>0.

Then the first derivative follows from directly differentiating the representation (
B.2
).

(c) This follows by direct computation. The exponential term ensures that the derivative of the finite-volume pressure is increasing in
\alpha
. As long as the box
\Lambda_{N}
has finite volume one can give the average particle density any pre-assigned value by choosing a chemical potential.

(d) The limit in (
B.4
) is obtained by direct calculation in conjunction with basic properties of the Bose function summarised in Appendix
A
. The convergence of the unique root is ensured as long as the expected particle density stays below the critical density which is finite only in dimensions
d\geq 3
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
B.5
.
Since
p\left(\beta,s\right)=+\infty
for
s>0
, we only need to search
s\leq 0
. On the interior of this region
p
is differentiable, and we look for stationary points. If
\varrho\geq\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}
, then there are no stationary points for
s<0
and
s\varrho-p\left(\beta,s\right)
is increasing in
s
. Hence the supremum is achieved at
s=0
. If
\varrho<\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}
, then there is a unique stationary point. This is also a local maximum and is given at
s=\alpha
as required. This has the required limit as
\varrho\uparrow\varrho_{\mathrm{c}}
implying the continuity for
f
. ∎

Appendix C
Lambert W function

The Lambert W function (sometimes called elsewhere the
Omega function
) is defined as the multi-valued inverse of the
\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
function
w\mapsto w{\rm e}^{w}
. We shall only be concerned with the two branches on
\mathbb{R}
. Figure
7
shows these two real branches, denoted
W_{0}
and
W_{-1}
. The
W_{0}
branch is defined on
\left[-{\rm e}^{-1},\infty\right)
, whereas the
W_{-1}
branch is only defined on
\left[-{\rm e}^{-1},0\right)
. Given a branch
W_{l}
with
l\in\left\{0,-1\right\}
, we can find its (real) derivative
W^{\prime}_{l}
by differentiating the equation
W_{l}\left(x\right){\rm e}^{W_{l}\left(x\right)}=x
. This gives us

W^{\prime}_{l}\left(x\right)=\frac{1}{x}\frac{W_{l}\left(x\right)}{1+W_{l}\left(x\right)}.

Taking further derivatives and applying induction shows that the branches are smooth on the interior of their respective domains, and gives expressions for each order of the derivative. We make use of some asymptotic expansions of
W_{0}
and
W_{1}
:

\displaystyle W_{0}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=x-x^{2}+o\left(x^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\text{as }x\to 0,

\displaystyle W_{0}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\log x-\log\left(\log x\right)+o\left(1\right)

\displaystyle\text{as }x\to+\infty,

\displaystyle W_{-1}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left(-x\right)-\log\left(-\log\left(-x\right)\right)+o\left(1\right)

\displaystyle\text{as }x\uparrow 0.

For more details, see
[
CGHJK96
]
.

x
W\left(x\right)
W_{0}
W_{-1}
-1/e
-1

Figure 7
.
The two real branches of
W
:
W_{0}
and
W_{-1}
.
